#278. Baked by Melissa enters Target with first at-home cupcake mix

Baked by Melissa will enter the at-home baking aisle in Target stores as the retailer tries to persuade shoppers to spend more at its locations this holiday season.

As of Sunday, Target is exclusively selling the brand's cupcake kits, liners and a cupcake pan, all in the dessert company's signature bite-size portions. The line includes three cupcake flavors: Christmas Tie-Dye, Marshmallow Hot Cocoa and Red Velvet.

The company's entry into at-home baking comes as a number of restaurants turn to consumer packaged goods to boost sales in an otherwise challenging time for the industry.

Some players, such as California Pizza Kitchen and TGI Fridays, have licensed their brands for frozen foods for decades. But other restaurant chains, including Chick-fil-A and Dutch Bros, have leaned into grocery products much more recently. For smaller companies, such as Momofuku or Carbone, space on a grocery shelf helps extend brand reach and gives consumers a chance to try their food without a reservation and at a much lower cost.

Founded by Melissa Ben-Ishay in 2008, Baked by Melissa has nine permanent locations in the New York area and ships its minicupcakes nationwide.

The regional chain has garnered attention outside the city thanks to Ben-Ishay's social media presence. On TikTok, the Baked by Melissa account has more than 2.8 million followers, fueled by popular recipe videos, such as for her green goddess salad dressing. Ben-Ishay's social media stardom has led to collaborations with Ferrero and Wonder.

Baked by Melissa had discussed at-home baking products for years, but Ben-Ishay, who is also president of the company, only decided to take the plunge a year ago during a trip to Target with her two kids, she told CNBC. A spin through the baking aisle made her decide Baked by Melissa "should be here," she said.

"I love baking cake mix. I truly do. And, quite frankly, sometimes I just think it tastes better," Ben-Ishay said.

But retail moves more slowly than Baked by Melissa's usual pace.

The dessert company needed just a few days to create New York Knicks-branded minicupcakes after the team won the NBA championship in June, for example, but figuring out retail products meant "a lot of waiting and approvals and new partners," Ben-Ishay said.

Twelve months later, the cupcake mixes have arrived on store shelves, just in time for the all-important holiday season.

For Target, the next two months will be critical as the retailer tries to woo back its core customer after a disappointing holiday season last year. That prototypical Target shopper often looks a lot like Ben-Ishay.

"We started with Target because I'm a 42-year-old mom of two, and I find myself there a lot," she said.

#267. Ferrari raises 2026 guidance after Q3 earnings beat

Ferrari on Tuesday raised its 2026 guidance after the sports car maker slightly beat Wall Street's third-quarter expectations.

Here's how Ferrari performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Ferrari raised its guidance to revenue of 7.65 billion euros, up from 7.6 billion euros; adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization to 3 billion euros, up from 2.97 billion euros; and adjusted EPS to at least 9.85 euros, up from at least 9.68 euros.

The automaker also upgraded its expectations for industrial free cash flow to up to 1.65 billion euros, up from previous expectations for at least 1.55 billion euros.

This is the second consecutive quarter Ferrari has raised its 2026 guidance, despite vehicle shipments being lower by 318 units through the first nine months of the year, at 10,170 vehicles, compared with last year. The decline is led by fewer shipments to the Americas and China, Hong Kong and Taiwan.

Ferrari said Tuesday that the guidance improvements were a result of stronger-than-expected customer personalizations, lower than anticipated currency headwinds and a second place finish in the Formula 1 championship.

"Our order book now reaches into 2028, and we still have two more launches to come this year. In addition, we continue to create engaging events around the world to further tighten the bonds with our clients and partners across Racing, Sports Cars and Lifestyle," Ferrari CEO Benedetto Vigna said in a release.

RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan said Tuesday that Ferrari's results were largely in line with Wall Street expectations. However, he said shares may be "under slight pressure given some were hoping for a bigger guidance raise, but expect it to get better once folks realize these results are healthy."

Ferrari's U.S. stock initially declined on the results before turning positive to close Tuesday at $414.77 a share, up 2.9%.

#259. Barkin sees interest rate hikes ahead, but at a gradual pace

Richmond Federal Reserve President Thomas Barkin said Thursday that he expects the central bank will have to raise interest rates further, though he's not sure by how much or at what pace.

In a CNBC interview that was part of the Richmond Fed's 2026 CFO Summit, the policymaker expressed concern over inflation and said monetary policy will need to adjust.

"Unemployment's near record lows, and inflation is too high. That means the path is pretty straightforward. The question, of course, is pace," Barkin said in the talk with CNBC's Steve Liesman.

"Gradual makes sense if you think there's uncertainty in the world, and if you think that there's information you're going to get in the period. And I think that's still a pace that makes sense to me," he added.

Barkin's comments come a little more than a week after his colleagues voted to keep the Fed's benchmark overnight borrowing rate anchored in a range between 3.75% and 4%. While Barkin is not a voting member on the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee now, he will be in 2027.

In its post-meeting statement, the FOMC said it is awaiting the "accumulation of more information" as it deliberates where to go next. When the committee OK'd a quarter percentage point hike in September, officials largely agreed that another increase was likely before the end of 2026.

Markets are pricing in a better than 80% chance of a hike in December, then one more move in 2027, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool.

Barkin said he considers current policy close to a point where it neither restricts nor promotes growth, which officials consider a neutral rate.

"If you're restricting the economy, presumably you're restricting consumption, which is you know roughly two-thirds of the economy, and that's relatively elevated," he said. "So that would make the case that we're a little bit accommodative."

Inflation, he added, "is the most important risk right now ... and I hope that our move in September and whatever else we might choose to do in the future will have some impact on inflation."

Artificial intelligence is one source of price pressures, though he said it eventually will spur production and help ease inflation further down the road.

In a related matter, he said the debt issuance to hyperscalers, estimated this year at better than $500 billion, is "worth watching" for potential credit issues.

"It's pretty hard to convince yourself that some of these big hyperscalers who are generating so much cash from their core businesses aren't good credits," he said. "So it's more who are the intermediaries in this world who are taking on risks that you don't know about. ... You do wonder where in the intermediary sector is the biggest risk."

#258. Versant earnings beat expectations as digital platforms drive growth

Versant Media on Tuesday reported quarterly earnings and revenue that beat expectations and reaffirmed its full-year 2026 guidance, citing strength across its portfolio and growth in its platforms revenue.

The media company reported $245 million in revenue from digital platforms including Fandango, marking a nearly 10% year-over-year increase. Versant said Fandango saw "significant growth" in ticketing revenue in the third quarter, notching its highest level since the pandemic.

Advertising revenue, however, slipped to $373 million during the period, down 5.3% from last year, primarily due to the impact of last year's Ryder Cup, according to the company.

The Versant portfolio includes CNBC, MS NOW, Golf Channel and other cable and digital properties.

Here's how the company performed in its third quarter, compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Versant completed four major distribution renewals this year following its spinoff from Comcast's NBCUniversal.

During the third quarter, Versant said, its networks reached more than 120 million viewers monthly and its aggregate viewership grew by double digits year over year. The company also launched MS NOW's direct-to-consumer product and Fandango's on-demand product as new growth drivers. CNBC is expected to launch a direct-to-consumer product next year, Versant said.

For the period ended Sept. 30, Versant recorded net income of $47 million, or 34 cents per share, compared with $80 million, or 56 cents per share, the year prior.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization was $353 million, beating Wall Street estimates of $305 million, according to StreetAccount.

Revenue fell slightly to $1.59 billion from $1.66 billion in the year-ago period. The company said its revenue figure included the acquisition of golf simulation company Full Swing and the sale of sports management software company SportsEngine.

The company reaffirmed its full-year revenue outlook of between $6.2 billion and $6.45 billion and its adjusted EBITDA outlook of $1.9 billion to $2.05 billion. It adjusted part of its full-year guidance, saying it now expects free cash flow to exceed the prior range of between $1 billion and $1.2 billion.

"We're building on strong relationships we already have with consumers, advertisers and distributors," Lazarus said on the call. "We're finding new ways to reach and serve those audiences. We have the brands, the financial strength and the people to execute this transition successfully."

Versant began trading as a standalone company on the Nasdaq in January after separating from Comcast. Though it began largely as a portfolio of cable networks, Versant executives have said they intend to diversify the company's revenue mix to eventually draw 50% from digital, platform, subscription, ad-supported and transactional businesses.

Live sports continues to be a major driver for the company. Versant said it saw "robust audience engagement" in the third quarter and that WNBA viewership on Versant's USA Sports networks was 25% higher than the prior year's cable average.

Versant also announced an additional $100 million accelerated stock repurchase program beginning Wednesday. It expects that transaction to be completed during the fourth quarter.

Disclosure: Versant Media is the parent company of CNBC.

#256. HSA-eligible health plans in 2027: What to know about tax savings vs. higher deductibles

Healthcare affordability was part of the midterm election cost-of-living debate.

It is uncertain how a new Congress might try to address that pain point. Now, as workers choose their 2027 health insurance coverage, millions face a more immediate decision: whether to pick a plan that lets them contribute to a health savings account, or HSA.

The question is whether an HSA-eligible plan with lower premiums and tax benefits is worth the higher deductible and potential out-of-pocket costs.

One noted advantage of an HSA is its tax benefits.

"You have a triple tax advantage," said Kalei Cadinha-Pua'a, vice chair and CEO of Honolulu-based Cadinha & Co., which ranked No. 21 on this year's CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Employer and employee contributions go into the fund before taxes, earnings in the account aren't taxed while they remain in the HSA, and withdrawals for qualified medical expenses are tax-free.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

Financial advisors say employees often overlook another potential tax savings. HSA contributions made through an employer's Section 125 cafeteria plan generally aren't subject to Social Security and Medicare taxes.

That can make payroll contributions more tax-efficient than contributing on your own and claiming a deduction later, said certified financial planner and enrolled agent Andy Panko, the founder of Tenon Financial in Metuchen, New Jersey.

Workers should check how their employer processes contributions and ask if they receive the Social Security and Medicare tax break on HSA contributions.

A worker who chooses to contribute to an HSA outside of payroll may be able to deduct eligible contributions on their federal tax return but generally won't get the break on Social Security and Medicare taxes.

HSAs are typically paired with a high-deductible health plan, so consumers generally have to be enrolled in a high-deductible health plan in order to contribute to the HSA.

Such a plan may have lower premiums relative to others available to consumers, but workers can face higher costs on the back end when they need care.

For 2027, the IRS says an HSA-eligible plan must generally have a deductible of at least $1,750 for individual coverage or $3,500 for family coverage. The plan's annual out-of-pocket expenses, excluding premiums, cannot exceed $8,700 for individual coverage or $17,400 for family coverage.

More employers are offering health plans with an HSA option. Across all U.S. health plan sponsors with at least 50 employees, 63% offer a high-deductible plan that qualifies for an HSA, according to a recent Marsh employer survey. More than a third, 37%, of all employees covered by an employer health plan are enrolled in an HSA-eligible plan.

"Employers often offer an HSA plan as a low-cost option, and then because of the higher deductible and the overall cost-sharing structure, these plans are typically costing less for the employer as well as the employee," said Frank Easley, senior vice president of Aon Health Solutions.

An HSA's tax advantages don't by themselves make an HSA-eligible plan the right choice. Workers should consider how the plan's premiums, deductible and out-of-pocket maximum align with expected healthcare needs before enrolling, financial advisors say.

They should also factor in any employer HSA contributions and the cash they have available to pay an unexpected medical bill before they reach their deductible, says Nathan Lochstampfor, CFP, an associate consultant at Harrisburg, Pennsylvania-based Conrad Siegel Investment Advisors, which is ranked No. 45 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 for 2026.

If consumers can't afford to pay the deductible of a high-deductible health plan, it may make sense to choose a plan with higher premiums and lower out-of-pocket costs if they need care, he said.

Lochstampfor recommends clients ask themselves: "If I got in a car accident going home today from work, could I afford that deductible? Or would I be better off maybe paying a little bit of higher premiums until I build up those cash reserves?"

HSA contribution limits for 2027 are $4,500 for individuals and $9,000 for families. People age 55 and older can make a $1,000 catch-up contribution.

You can use an HSA to pay for qualified medical expenses now or later. Any money left in the account generally carries over from year to year, and earnings aren't taxed while they remain in the account. HSA balances can also be invested. As of midyear, 11% of HSA accounts held invested assets, according to Devenir, an HSA investment firm.

Ideally, financial advisors say, you keep some of the balance in cash to cover short-term needs and invest the rest for retirement.

"If you have a long time and you can begin to develop a strategy for savings, it can really work and benefit you in the future when you don't have that earned income to pay for your healthcare needs," Cadinha-Pua'a said.

Some HSA owners pay current medical expenses out of pocket and leave money invested in the account so it can grow tax-free. They can use their HSA funds to reimburse themselves years later for those out-of-pocket expenses. Some conditions apply: It must be a qualified medical expense and incurred after the HSA was established, for example.

That strategy can be smart — but it requires good records.

The IRS wants to make sure expenses aren't double counted. Account holders need to keep records showing that any HSA distributions paid or reimbursed qualified medical expenses weren't also claimed as an itemized deduction.

"Be really honest with yourself," Cadinha-Pua'a said. "Are you a good record keeper?"

She recommends making digital copies of receipts, bills and proof of payment, organizing them in an electronic folder and backing up that folder to the cloud. That can help preserve the documentation if a computer fails or the account holder needs to substantiate a reimbursement years later.

#253. Yum Brands earnings beat estimates, but cyclospora outbreak weighs on Taco Bell

Yum Brands on Tuesday reported earnings that beat expectations, but a cyclospora outbreak linked to iceberg lettuce at Taco Bell weighed on the company's same-store sales growth.

However, Yum said Taco Bell, which the company considers a "growth engine" for its business, has since rebounded.

"Since the end of September, Taco Bell U.S. has been outpacing the [quick-service restaurant] industry on same-store sales growth," Yum CEO Chris Turner said in a statement.

Taco Bell's same-store sales are up about 1% so far in the fourth quarter, CFO Ranjith Roy said on the company's earnings conference call Tuesday. In the last four weeks ended Friday, same-store sales have risen 3% compared with the year-ago period.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Yum reported fiscal third-quarter net income of $1.62 billion, or $5.92 per share, up from $397 million, or $1.41 per share, a year earlier.

Excluding gains from its divestiture of Pizza Hut and other items, the restaurant company earned $1.56 per share.

During the quarter, Yum completed its divestiture of Pizza Hut. It closed the sale of the pizza chain's business in mainland China to Yum China on Aug. 7 and sold the rest of Pizza Hut to LongRange Capital on Sept. 1. The company's third-quarter results include Pizza Hut up until those dates.

Excluding Pizza Hut, Yum's same-store sales were flat for the quarter as Taco Bell weighed on the company's overall results. Taco Bell's same-store sales declined by 4% as diners temporarily pulled back on visits due to the foodborne illness outbreak.

Yum executives previously said the weekend of July 18 saw the most severe impact to sales, but trends have improved since then.

Taco Bell's most loyal customers returned faster, Yum said, thanks in part to weekly Tuesday deals for loyalty program members after the cyclospora outbreak took over headlines.

Traffic from "medium" and "light" customers has bounced back to about 95% of prior levels, while visits from "heavy" users is back to normal, Roy said. To attract more casual diners, Taco Bell has been relying on star power, like a collaboration with pop star Olivia Rodrigo on a pink-colored Mountain Dew Baja Blast.

Outside of the U.S., Taco Bell's much smaller international business reported same-store sales growth of 5%.

KFC's same-store sales ticked up 1%, helped by system sales growth in China and Europe. The U.S., now the chicken chain's third-largest market, saw system sales shrink 6% in the quarter.

Like Restaurant Brands International's Popeyes, KFC has struggled in the U.S. amid a wave of new competitors and more budget-conscious consumers. Its home market represents only about 5% of KFC's operating profit, but it remains a symbolically important part of the business for Yum investors.

During the quarter, KFC U.S. launched popcorn chicken, which sold out in many restaurants. However, Yum said the limited-time menu item was not enough to offset the successful Hot & Spicy Wings and Potato Wedges promotion during the year-ago period.

Globally, KFC is trying to become "the most modern chicken QSR," Turner said on the company's earnings conference call. To achieve that lofty goal, he said the chain will roll out buzzy promotions, a new brand identity, and a wider menu that includes upgraded sauces and more beverages.

#252. Blue Origin pushes launch to January 2027 as it races to rebuild damaged launchpad

Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin has delayed a return to flight for its flagship New Glenn rocket to January 2027, later than its earlier target of the end of this year, as it rushes to recover from a major explosion in May.

Blue Origin CEO Dave Limp told employees in a memo Wednesday that the company's launch timing slipped as a result of delays with reconstructing its Cape Canaveral launchpad, including due to bad weather over the summer.

"This small adjustment doesn't diminish what we've accomplished or the importance of the work still ahead," Limp wrote in the memo, which was viewed by CNBC. "Returning New Glenn to flight remains our top priority — it's worth it."

The Financial Times earlier reported the memo. The January 2027 time frame is about 10 days later than Blue Origin's earlier launch target, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC. The person asked not to be named because the matter is internal.

The New Glenn explosion in May, which occurred during a hot-fire test, badly damaged Blue Origin's Florida launchpad. The company was slated to deploy 48 satellites for Amazon's Leo internet-from-space service just a few days later.

Since then, the company has moved swiftly to repair the launch facility and reworked its pad configuration. Bezos told Fox News last month that the company was still expecting New Glenn to fly again in December.

Limp said Blue Origin has made great progress on the recovery, but he noted that "rebuilding at this pace hasn't been easy."

"Your collective work on our return to flight has been nothing short of remarkable," Limp wrote. "In just six months, Team Blue has accomplished what many thought would take years."

The mishap was inconveniently timed, as Blue Origin has been working to ramp up its launch cadence in an effort to compete with its chief rival, Elon Musk's SpaceX, for commercial and government customers.

The New Glenn delays have hampered Amazon's Leo constellation buildout, for which it relies on Blue Origin for a portion of its launches. Amazon has turned to other providers, including United Launch Alliance and Arianespace, in the interim, especially as SpaceX has phased out Falcon 9 launches to prioritize testing its enormous Starship rocket.

NASA has also tapped Blue Origin for several contracts in the agency's Artemis return-to-the-moon program, including a lunar lander.

The company announced new investments in its Florida launch facilities last month with the goal of supporting multiple flights per week by the end of the decade.

#247. Voters say the economy drove them to the polls — and blame Trump for their troubles

As President Donald Trump campaigned for the nation's highest office in 2024, he regularly blamed Democrats for the country's economic anxiety.

During campaign stops across the U.S., Trump frequently asked voters if they were better off than they were four years prior. At the time, his message appeared to land: In an election centered on inflation and the cost of living, Trump won every swing state and the nationwide popular vote.

Two years later, voters grappling with those same kitchen-table issues took away much of his party's power in Washington and in states across the country.

Democrats rode a blue wave of historic proportions in Tuesday's midterm election to flip everything from battleground congressional seats to long-held Republican governorships. The Democratic Party will now have control of both the House and Senate, ending the Republican Party's trifecta of power in the federal government.

Nearly nine out of 10 voters in the CNBC Voter Poll said the cost of living was a crucial or major factor in their vote for Congress. Participants in the poll ranked the economy as the most important issue facing the country during the midterm election, ahead of topics like the state of democracy and immigration. Three out of every four survey participants rated the economy as "poor" or "not so good," compared with just 2% who described it as "excellent."

And perhaps most telling of all: Fewer than one out of every five voters said their family's financial standing was better than it was two years ago.

Much of their dissatisfaction was centered on Trump himself, with about two out of every three voters saying his policies worsened economic conditions in the country.

"It was all about the economy," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's Analytics. "Most Americans are struggling with the high inflation and have been for quite some time, and they're just angry."

Tuesday's election played out against the backdrop of a war with Iran that has been politically unpopular and exacerbated the inflationary pressures facing everyday Americans. The CNBC Voter Poll found nearly three out of every four people said the war was a crucial or major factor in their vote for Congress.

Trump told Fox News that voters were presumably unhappy with the surge in gas prices, even if they generally agreed with the need for military action against Iran. Vice President JD Vance said on social media that some voters who backed him and Trump in 2024 likely supported Democrats in the midterms as a result of the war.

U.S. drivers paid about $4.15 for an average gallon of gas on Tuesday, the highest of any midterm or presidential election day since at least 2000, according to AAA. Gas prices have surged about 37% over the past year as the Middle East conflict crippled the global crude trade.

"America sent a very clear message," said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at GasBuddy, a fuel price tracking platform. "Going into an election with prices that on Election Day have never been higher is something that garners a lot of frustration from consumers."

Higher prices for gas and other energy products have cost the average American household nearly $1,230 since the start of the Iran war, according to an analysis from Zandi. The financial pain felt by U.S. consumers from the conflict has spilled into other areas. Rising borrowing costs and expanded military services roughly cost households more than $900 in addition, according to Zandi.

The consumer price index, a broad basket of goods and services tracked by the Bureau of Labor Statistics, reaccelerated during the war with Iran. Increases in consumer prices have outpaced wage gains in recent months, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Prices for diesel, the fuel type used by truckers and trains, climbed to records that transportation executives have said feel "like science fiction." In the latest producer price index report, the BLS said that rising diesel costs pushed up prices for many goods.

The CNBC Voter Poll found more than three out of every five people believed that Trump's policies led to higher prices. Almost nine out of 10 said they were very or somewhat concerned about the prices for a variety of goods such as utilities, food and oil. The poll was a collaborative effort across several news organizations that drew on about 100,000 interviews conducted by phone, online and in-person at polling locations with a margin of error of 0.6%.

Trump took over an economy coming out of the pandemic that was caught in a "vibecession," the term used to describe the disconnect between sour consumer confidence and positive economic fundamentals. With consumer prices now on one of their longest streaks of higher-than-preferred inflation readings on record, the average American's economic sentiment is even worse today.

The University of Michigan's consumer sentiment survey in 2026 fell to the worst levels in its seven-decade history. The Conference Board, meanwhile, said its consumer confidence gauge in September slid to 14-year lows.

Inflation pushed voters to a "breaking point," said Francesco D'Acunto, a Georgetown University professor who researches behavioral economics. "If consumers become angry voters, they vote against the status quo — in this case, the Trump administration."

Before the U.S. began its war with Iran in late February, economists and monetary policymakers were already sounding the alarm about what Trump's policies could mean for the economy.

Trump pitched his plan for broad and steep tariffs as a way to revive domestic manufacturing, but the policy sent financial markets reeling and raised the prospect of a potential recession. Researchers at the New York Federal Reserve said that several consumer prices would have fallen if not for Trump's tariffs, many of which were later struck down by the Supreme Court.

In the CNBC Voter Poll, 64% said Trump's tariff policies hurt the economy in their area. Just 14% said they helped.

Trump's White House also oversaw mass deportations in major cities that gave way to large-scale protests and the death of two U.S. citizens by federal immigration agents. A University of Pennsylvania study found that the cities experiencing immigration enforcement surges collectively saw decreasing foot traffic and billions of dollars in lost consumer spending.

About three out of five voters said they disapproved of how Immigration and Customs Enforcement handled its job and that the Trump administration had gone too far on the issue, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Still, voters were more likely to say the Republican Party was better at handling immigration.

To be sure, some policies under Trump's White House have been better received. The Internal Revenue Service said in October that the average tax refund grew by 11% following the passage of Trump's "big beautiful bill." So-called Trump Accounts are allowing parents to tap into government and donor funds to build nest eggs for children.

But on Tuesday, voters showed that affordability remained a leading concern as the Iran war continues and inflation pinches pocketbooks. What changed compared with the 2024 presidential election was who they believed could fix it.

In 2024, more voters said they thought Trump was better equipped to handle the economy than former Vice President Kamala Harris. This week, voters said Democrats were better suited than Republicans to manage the economy.

But the party will have a lot of work to do. Seven out of 10 respondents in the CNBC Voter Poll said they felt the U.S. economic system generally favored the wealthy.

"We know pretty clearly what is making Americans upset," said Zandi, who has advised politicians from both parties. "There's another election coming up in two years. Hopefully, policymakers are listening to all this."

#243. Why Gen Z are planning for life without a state pension

Why Gen Z are planning for life without a state pension

Joel has finally landed his first graduate engineering job after several years of lowerâpaid roles. He's in his early 20s, lives with his parents and works in London. But instead of splashing the extra cash, or saving up for holidays or a house deposit, he's decided to squirrel more of it away into his workplace pension.

The reason? He doesn't think he'll get any kind of state pension. Like Joel, around half of Gen Z (those born from 1997â2012) say they don't expect the state pension to exist by the time they retire. It's pretty stark to hear, but growing up with constant headlines about an ageing population, a proportionally smaller working-age population, and the pressure that government finances are under, Joel thinks it's his generation that will suffer.

"I don't believe that I'll be a recipient of a state pension. I know a lot of people my age don't think they're going to be... There just won't be enough money," he says.

Retirement has always felt distant when you're in your 20s - something to think about later. But what's emerging among today's underâ30s is something different: not just distance, but doubt.

"It just mathematically doesn't make senseâ¦ There has to get to a point where that state pension is taking up too much of the budget and can't exist in the way that it exists right now," Joel says.

"I don't believe that I'll be a recipient of a state pension," says Joel

The state pension age is shifting. At the start of April, the age at which you receive it began to gradually creep up, rising from 66 years to 67 years by March 2028. It's due to go up again in 20 years' time to 68, though that might happen earlier as the government has an ongoing independent review.

That's a frustration for 27-year-old retail manager Connor, who got in touch via BBC Your Voice, because he says "the goalpost keeps moving". "At the minute I'll be 68 by the time I can retire, but I do think I'll be probably closer to 75, if I'm honest."

More than 13 million people - 19% of the population - are currently of state pension age. By 2050, even with the state pension age rising to 68, that group is projected to exceed 15 million people, nearly a quarter of the population, with numbers projected to climb towards 17 million by the 2070s. In other words, there will be lots more people qualifying for the state pension, and fewer working people, as a proportion, paying taxes into the pot to cover the bill.

At the same time, almost half of workingâage adults are not paying into a private pension pot. That means many will be relying solely on the state pension for their retirement income - and with relative poverty rates among pensioners now at 14%, we can already see how difficult that can be.

Experts warn that if a whole generation stops believing the state pension will be there, it could push people towards more risky investments, prompt overly restrictive behaviour, or lead others not to save at all.

So, are we heading towards a major pension crisis for many in Gen Z? And if we are, might the Gen Z generation end up redefining what retirement looks like?

For those hitting the state pension milestone today, as long as they've made 35 years of National Insurance contributions, they're entitled to Â£241.30 a week.

That amount rises each year to help people keep pace with rising living costs. Since 2011, pension increases have been guaranteed by the triple lock - which means the rise will either match the rate of inflation, average earnings or 2.5% - whichever is higher.

But in recent weeks, several organisations have called for the rules to be rewritten.

The centre-left Resolution Foundation think tank has argued for scrapping the triple lock, saying that continuing to prioritise the incomes of pensioners over working-age adults and children would be unfair.

Meanwhile, the Tony Blair Institute (TBI), a think tank set up by the former prime minister, takes things a step further and has called for the whole state pension to be scrapped and replaced with a new "Lifespan Fund". Thomas Smith, director of economic policy at the TBI, argued: "Britain's state pension system was built for a different era. We can't keep pouring money into a system that is increasingly unaffordable."

It suggests scrapping the triple lock and allowing people to access some of their state pension early if they need it because of redundancy and frequent job changes.

That's an idea that might appeal to Connor in Chesterfield. He's facing redundancy from his job at a global cosmetics firm, and the ability to draw down a small amount - effectively a withdrawal from his future state pension - might tide him through.

"There's not that many jobs out there at the minute, unfortunately. I still live at home with my parents luckily, but I pay board to them. I still have a car payment, I still have my insurance to pay."

"There's not that many jobs out there," says Connor

But former pensions minister Steve Webb argues those changes would be "a huge backward step". He says the benefit of the current system is its simplicity which shouldn't be replaced with "something fiendishly complex and highly intrusive, which would take many decades to implement in full."

The government says it has committed to the triple lock for the rest of this parliament, and that the Pensions Commission, an independent body set up to review the regime for UK private pensions, is examining "how we can ensure secure retirements for tomorrow's pensioners".

It's likely that those in their 20s will not have a tripleâlocked pension. That means living on the state pension alone will become more difficult, as its value may rise more slowly than the cost of food, travel, clothing and household bills.

For those who doubt the triple lock will endure, the debate often shifts to how the state pension can be sustained at all. One idea that regularly surfaces is meansâtesting. In some ways it already is: very lowâincome pensioners can receive an additional benefit called Pension Credit. But 24âyearâold Joel believes that for the state pension to survive, the choices may need to be more radical.

"I don't think a means-tested state pension is necessarily a bad thing. But it would be a bad thing if it only applies to people in 50 years and not now when we should be saving some of that money."

Engineer Joel is one of life's squirrels. His fear about the future of the state pension means he's doubling-down on his private pension. He contacted BBC Your Voice because he feels that consecutive governments have sheltered current pensioners, leaving the consequences for his generation to face.

"I'm going to have to increase the amount of my paycheck that goes into a private pension, which obviously isn't good with cost of living through the roof," he says.

The scale of what younger workers may need to save adds to that anxiety for some. Investment company Rathbones estimates that a single person retiring today at 65 (with the state pension) may need around Â£796,000 in savings to fund a "comfortable retirement". If the state pension remains, a 25âyearâold today would need a pot of around Â£1.68m to retire comfortably as a single person. Without the state pension, the figure for Gen Z jumps to more than Â£2.4m.

Against this backdrop, Joel says many of his friends are considering opting out of private and workplace pensions altogether and investing independently instead, mostly in "crypto or index funds and things like that.

"There's a sense, whether it's right or wrong, that that's more secure than putting it in a pension where they're also going to take a chip on top."

It's possible that individual investment choices could earn more than a pension scheme, but it's a big gamble.

Behavioural economics suggests that when people lose trust in a system, they tend to either opt out entirely or overâcompensate. Both can be problematic. Saving extra in a private pension may limit current life options, but opting out can leave many with riskier retirement savings, or indeed none at all.

How the social media ban could reshape how all of us use the internet

The pressure on the Church of England to ditch its slavery reparations plan

What one country's experiment says about attempts to boost birth rates

In central Manchester, 23âyearâold Ashleigh agrees with Joel that the state pension is unlikely to be coming her way: "At this rate I don't think anyone's ever going to retire, I think everyone will just have to fend for themselves in the end."

But as someone on a lower income, her pension choices are less squirrelâlike. When working for a big retailer, she says that she chose to stop contributing to her employer's auto-enrolment pension.

"I opted out of it. I need the money now." She explains: "I'd rather save for a house and then at least I have something to show for it".

Some experts warn that the gap between rich and poor in retirement could widen significantly for this generation.

Dr Suzy Morrissey, deputy director at the Pensions Policy Institute (PPI), believes that alongside how much Gen Z save privately, another factor will widen the divide: far more of them will be renting.

"Renting in retirement increases your chances of pensioner poverty, and they do face challenges to save, as younger people, that previous generations didn't face when they were at the same age," she says. "If we have people paying rent in retirement who don't have large pension pots to cover those expenses, then that equals higher risk of pensioner poverty."

But Morrissey sees a silver lining: pensions auto-enrollment, the system that automatically puts most employees into a workplace pension unless they opt out. If they've been employees, "they will have spent their working life contributing into a pension pot, and they will be the first generation that will have spent their whole life doing that."

It'll be a backstop for many, but the minimum contribution rate is unlikely to be enough for a comfortable retirement. It's not automatic for the self-employed and people like Ashleigh have opted out because of immediate financial pressures, so it looks like plenty won't see the benefit of that silver lining.

For some, the response to an uncertain future is to focus on the present.

Lauren, from Hull, says: "Money always comes back, time doesn't. The world is so vast, we shouldn't wait till the last 10/20 years of our lives to go out and see it!"

At 24, she's about to take six months off from her job as a business coordinator. She's one of a growing number planning to take regular career breaks, or "grown-up gap years", which many are terming "mini retirement". HSBC's 2025 UK survey found that 63% of Gen Z plan to take at least one miniâretirement, compared with 32% of Gen X and 13% of Boomers.

"The majority of my friends don't pay into pensions and instead decide to take their whole wage [after taxes] and spend it how they see fit. A large proportion goes on travels or holidays," says Lauren.

"I currently don't pay into a pension, actually I never have. I'd way rather have my money now and use it to live life," she says.

"The majority of my friends don't pay into pensions," says Lauren

But there's a warning for Gen Z from the experience of the Waspi women - hundreds of thousands born in the 1950s who campaigners say have suffered because of poorly communicated rises in the state pension age. Their financial shock shows that costs may only become clear when it's too late to course-correct.

If Gen Z's suspicions are right, and the state pension becomes a less dependable part of income in later life, then more will have to take a totally different approach to retirement, savings and life choices to navigate the new landscape.

Additional reporting: Kris Bramwell and Harriet Whitehead

#242. Why the UK is dithering over what to do about e-scooters

Why the UK is dithering over what to do about e-scooters

When I step out of Southampton Central railway station, it takes me just seconds to spot an e-scooter. In this city, these devices have become a normal part of the transport ecosystem, along with e-bikes.

"It's just the most convenient way to commute from home to the station and back," says Keith Reed, who I speak to as he arrives at the station to catch a train to London. Keith says uses rental e-scooters twice a week. His alternative option would be his bike, but he'd then have to worry about where to leave it.

Around the city, there are parking areas for bright orange rental devices everywhere - but they're not necessarily a permanent feature. Southampton hosts one of the numerous government-backed trials around England. This one has been going on since 2021.

A study suggests around half of users of e-scooter do so in conjunction with another mode of transport - like Keith, who rides to the railway station

It also doesn't take long to find e-scooters which aren't part of the trial, but instead are owned by individuals. I see a man riding one along the pavement outside a parade of shops. He doesn't want to stop and talk.

These privately owned e-scooters are illegal to ride on public roads across the UK, but they've proliferated regardless. One expert told me the situation was a "Wild West".

Among people I spoke to in Southampton, the same concerns came up time and time again: "They're a blinking nuisance," says elderly pedestrian Fred. Another shopper, Martin, says he's "very concerned" whenever he sees them racing through precincts: "An accident's almost inevitable."

The endgame of the trials is to reach a decision on whether to make permanent hired e-scooter schemes such as the one Keith uses - and whether to bring in legislation that would legalise and regulate privately owned e-scooters too.

But six years since the trials started around England, there's still no conclusion - and as the UK government has responsibility for road traffic legislation across Great Britain, officials in Scotland and Wales say they are waiting on Westminster to act.

So why is it taking so long for the government to decide what to do, and what are the consequences of delay? What does the evidence tell us about how safe these things are?

And why are people using them in the first place - are they here to stay as a useful part of the transport offer, or will they end up being viewed as a pest on our streets?

You'd recognise an e-scooter if you saw one. The Department for Transport's (DfT's) definition of an e-scooter includes no pedals, two wheels and a maximum continuous power rating of 500W.

And so far, the trials around England appear to have proved popular. According to DfT research, fom January 2022 to May 2024, 36.9 million trips were made across 30 trial areas outside of London. On average, nearly 19,500 e-scooters were available to hire every day.

Dr Yasir Ali, a reader in Transport Modelling at Loughborough University, says e-scooters are "complementary" to other modes of transport, rather than replacing them. Due to factors such as range and being exposed to the elements, they are less suitable for longer trips, rural areas with less infrastructure or in bad weather. They work best as "a first- and last-mile solution" in urban areas, to reduce reliance on cars for short trips.

Shared mobility charity CoMoUK's 2025 report suggests that for more than half of the people using rental e-scooters, the most common use was travelling to work or school. Its analysis of DfT data suggests the majority of people using hired e-scooters are male and under 35.

"Sometimes it's a bit difficult to take the bus from dot to dot", says Yan Zhang, who works at Southampton university and doesn't drive, "so for shorter distances, I'll take the scooter."

Like Yan Zhang, e-scooter users are less likely to have a car, CoMo's report says - this is partly explained by official trials' concentration in urban areas

Around half of respondents to CoMoUK's online survey used it in conjunction with another mode of transport - such as riding to a railway station, like Keith.

Earlier evidence from a national evaluation of the trials published in 2022 found that although some users would have driven instead had they not had access to an e-scooter, the biggest "modal shift" - meaning switch in ways of getting around - to e-scooters was from walking. Given walking is an active mode of travel, this has raised questions over how significant the public health impact of e-scooter use really is.

And the usage of privately owned e-scooters isn't reflected in this research. The DfT has said between 760,000 and 1.2 million people in England aged 16 and over own one - despite the fact that it remains illegal to ride them in public.

In the eyes of the law, e-scooters are classed as motor vehicles. Outside of the trials, they can't be insured, and thus can only legally be ridden on private land, with the owner's permission.

Parking bays for e-scooters and e-bikes are available across Southampton

The trials themselves are bound by strict rules. The devices' top speed is limited. Businesses who operate the pilots must insure them. Users must have a full or provisional driving licence, and they must not ride on the pavement. Helmets are recommended but not mandatory.

Richard Dilks, CEO of CoMoUK says "mass-scale use of something that is inherently low-carbon, low-impact, low-cost" is a "good thing" but "we also don't have rules of engagement about how they should be ridden".

And as a result of the delays in introducing regulation, Loughborogh University's Yasir Ali says "we are increasingly in a situation where the law and reality have diverged", with "the disadvantages of e-scooter use without securing the full benefits of regulation".

Back in Southampton, I meet a lady pushing a black e-scooter, who doesn't want to give her name. She says nobody told her when she bought it that it was illegal to ride on the streets.

The Driver and Vehicle Standards Agency has a unit that carries out surveillance of retailers to check they're complying with the law. But earlier this month, an investigation by the Press Association news agency discovered some retailers were advertising them for commuting purposes.

Police tell me they regularly see children illegally riding private e-scooters. Margaret Winchcomb, deputy executive director at the Parliamentary Advisory Council for Transport Safety (PACTS), says some are being specifically marketed at kids.

Police say they will seize privately owned e-scooters that are ridden illegally

So what can police do? Forces regularly launch crackdowns where they seize illegally used devices, and often crush them. Each year, awareness campaigns run urging parents not to buy their kids an e-scooter for Christmas. But enforcement is a challenge.

Insp Matt Boiles from Avon and Somerset's road policing team says problems arise even with the legal trial devices - for example, people riding them after drinking alcohol, which counts as drink-driving. Riding with another person on the back is another common issue.

But officers see private e-scooters being illegally ridden in public places "all the time, every single day" and they can be really hard to catch - it isn't always safe for police in a car to pursue someone on an e-scooter, he says.

Insp Boiles despairs over parents buying them for children: "We do have parents attend a police station quite a lot, getting very, very upset that their child's Â£500 e-scooter has been seized." But "the legislation is really, really clear that you can't have it back".

There's a potential risk of fire if unregulated devices with lithium batteries are left to charge unattended. Most train operators have banned e-scooters on board for this reason.

But there's also the fact that riders are unprotected in the case of falling off or being hit by motorised vehicles. And - along with e-bikes - they can end up involved in collisions where pedestrians are hurt. Many trial e-scooters in the UK are restricted to 12.5mph, but privately owned scooters' speeds aren't.

Some coroners have even raised concerns. In 2022, a coroner in London called for action after the death of 14-year-old Fatima Abukah on a privately owned scooter.

According to the DfT, in 2024 - the most recent year for which figures are available - 1,390 casualties were recorded in collisions that involved e-scooter in England, Scotland and Wales, three more than in the previous 12 months. The vast majority, 1,096, were e-scooter users themselves.

Young boys and men appear to be over-represented in the stats. Of the casualties, 302 - the most of any age and sex category - were males aged 10-19.

Six deaths were recorded in 2024, unchanged from the previous year. Five of these were riders and one a pedestrian.

Although the numbers aren't conclusive, it is thought more accidents are happening on privately owned e-scooters. Winchcomb says police statistics "aren't reflective of the number of injuries".

Nonetheless, campaigners believe there is enough evidence to show that regulation is urgently needed.

Carly Calland's 14-year-old son Jacob died in March 2025 of a catastrophic head injury. He was a passenger on an e-scooter that was involved with a collision with a car.

If privately owned e-scooters are legalised for public use, Carly believes, there should be mandatory helmets, a ban on carrying passengers and penalties for parents that allow children to ride illegally.

"If Jacob was wearing a helmet that day, he would still be here," Carly, from Wythenshawe in Greater Manchester, says.

Carly is not against e-scooters. They "are really good for people to get to work, and they are eco-friendly", she tells me, but "they just need to be used in the correct way."

What happens if the current situation goes on? Her answer is emphatic: "More deaths."

Meanwhile a number of major European cities have ended their shared e-scooter schemes. Paris abolished them after a referendum in 2023. Brussels is the most recent, announcing theirs would end in 2027, following a rise in accidents.

The last evaluation of the trials in England was in 2022. New findings are expected by early next year.

In recent years, successive British transport secretaries have supported the idea of a new law to legalise and regulate the e-scooters available for sale. E-scooters were mentioned in the 2022 Queen's speech, but nothing materialised.

The hidden cost of the night shift and how to sleep it off

After record heat, could the Atlantic make Britain's weather even more extreme?

We are living fewer years in good health: Is the NHS part of the problem?

Winchcomb says some of the "dithering" may be because of the potential knock-on effect of any legislation for users of mobility scooters.

There is some frustration from devolved administrations around the UK about the delays in Whitehall. The Scottish Government "will continue to urge the UK government, which holds powers on registration and licensing over off-road vehicles, to take action in this area", a spokesperson says.

The Welsh Government says it will "monitor developments on legalisation plans and consider the implications for Wales".

Northern Ireland's Department for Infrastructure says it is also monitoring the trials in England, as well as the impact of recent law changes in the Republic of Ireland.

The DfT says it says the trials are "building the real-world evidence needed to get regulation right" and it will consult on longer-term rules, including registration and licensing requirements, within the next year.

"E-scooters have real potential to improve local connectivity and cut emissions, and we want to see that potential realised safely and responsibly across the UK," a spokesperson says.

But Ali says: "The current halfway house becomes harder to justify as usage grows."

Even if legislation and associated technical standards were introduced, Richard Dilks acknowledges enforcing them would remain difficult.

But, he adds: "That problem only gets bigger if you leave it."

#240. Pfizer hikes full-year outlook on new product growth, taps company veteran as new CFO

Pfizer on Tuesday reported fiscal third-quarter results that topped estimates and hiked its full-year outlook, as demand for its newly launched and acquired products grows.

Pfizer also Tuesday announced that Cecile Guegan, who has held various finance leadership roles at the company over the last two decades, stepped in as chief financial officer this month. She had served as interim CFO since August, when Pfizer's former CFO, Dave Denton, stepped down to take the same position at Nike.

The company is now expecting full-year revenue to total $62 billion to $64 billion, which compares with a previous outlook of $60.5 billion to $62.5 billion. The low end of that sales range would be roughly flat or down slightly compared with 2025 revenue of $62.6 billion. Still, Pfizer cited its "strong year-to-date performance" as a driver for the guidance hike.

In an exclusive interview with CNBC, Pfizer CEO Albert Bourla said the company's Covid product revenue was "very low" this quarter and year, but called it "positive news for us."

"Covid revenues was an overhang on the performance of our stock because of the perception that it is volatile and it's material to us. Actually, we've proven that we can make it irrelevant," Bourla said. "If anything, only an upside because, in the lowest possible Covid performance, we are beating, and we are raising by $1.5 billion because of the overperformance of the other products."

Pfizer reaffirmed its Covid product outlook of roughly $4 billion for the year, expecting the majority of Covid shot sales in the fourth quarter during vaccination season, Guegan said during the call.

The pharmaceutical giant now expects full-year adjusted profit of between $2.95 and $3.05 per share. That compares with previous guidance of between $2.80 and $3 per share.

That includes a one-time $650 million charge, or a negative impact of 10 cents per share, recorded in the third quarter tied to the company's licensing and collaboration agreement with Chinese cancer drugmaker Innovent Biologics.

Pfizer is working on a comeback story after its massive pandemic-era success waned. As demand for Covid products drops and generic rivals threaten its biggest moneymakers, Bourla is looking to jump-start growth through strategic acquisitions, fresh cancer drugs and a big bet on weight loss treatments through its acquisition of Metsera.

"In addition to the revenues that they generate now, and they will continue generating so that we can overcome the patent, the loss of patent exclusivity, we are bringing pipeline that will perform even better in terms of growth," Bourla told CNBC, referring to Pfizer's launched and acquired products.

Pfizer in August expanded its multiyear cost-cutting efforts to help offset sharp declines in Covid product sales, and is now targeting roughly $9.7 billion in net savings through 2029.

Here's what the company reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Pfizer reported third-quarter revenue of $16.58 billion, relatively flat from the same period a year ago. The company cited an operational decrease of $57 million, primarily due to lower Covid revenues, and a $15 million negative impact from foreign exchange.

That was offset by strong sales of key products like its blood thinner Eliquis and targeted cancer drug Padcev, which jumped and beat expectations.

Eliquis in particular raked in $2.52 billion in sales, up 25%. Analysts were expecting revenue of $2.40 billion, according to StreetAccount. Padcev posted $720 million in revenue for the period, which compares with the $658 million that analysts were expecting.

Sales of Pfizer's newly launched and acquired products contributed $3.8 billion, growing 32% operationally, Guegan said during the earnings call. That includes Padcev, its respiratory syncytial virus vaccine Abrysvo, its pneumococcal shot Prevnar 20 and oral lung cancer drug Lorbrena, among several other products.

'We continue to invest behind this group of products to mitigate [loss of exclusivity] headwinds over the next several years," Guegan said. "In addition, this quarter's performance demonstrates clear progress against our objective of returning to growth toward the end of the decade."

Pfizer's Covid products also topped estimates for the quarter despite seeing steep sales declines. The company's Covid vaccine generated $808 million in sales, which compares with the $770.4 million that analysts were expecting, StreetAccount estimates show. Meanwhile, Pfizer's antiviral pill, Paxlovid, booked $449 million in sales, coming in above the $394.1 million that analysts were expecting.

The company booked net income of $3.24 billion, or 56 cents per share, for the period. That compares with net income of $3.54 billion, or 62 cents per share, during the third quarter of 2025.

Excluding certain items, including restructuring charges and costs associated with intangible assets, Pfizer posted earnings per share of 87 cents for the quarter.

#238. Aviation faces hotter, stormier skies – and passengers might have to accept more disruption

Aviation faces hotter, stormier skies â and passengers might have to accept more disruption

As the country sweltered through one of the many heatwaves of a baking summer in late June, towering thunderclouds were gathering over the south of England and across much of northern Europe. It was a Saturday, and with the holiday season getting under way, thousands of passengers were heading to London's Heathrow and Gatwick airports to catch flights abroad. It was always going to be a very busy day.

What followed, however, was chaos for travellers. As air traffic controllers struggled to route aircraft around the storms, airspace became heavily congested and many planes due to fly were simply forced to wait on the ground. In the end, some 900 flights to and from both airports were delayed, some by up to 11 hours. Dozens more were cancelled. At airports across Europe, passengers expecting to fly into London also faced long waits and uncertainty.

The weather in the UK may now have cooled, and passengers may soon forget their experiences. But some within the industry believe what happened in June should be seen as a warning.

The timing in this case was clearly unlucky. But there are concerns that such incidents are only going to become more common as the effects of climate change mount up around the world and weather systems are likely to become increasingly unstable, making disruption more likely and adding to costs for airlines, airports and travellers.

Tim Atkinson, an airline risk consultant and former pilot, says the very crowded skies over the UK and northern Europe mean the region is particularly exposed.

He thinks there is a real risk, for example, that all of London's airports could be affected by storms at the same time, potentially with major consequences in the air and on the ground.

"What we're seeing now is very rapid climate change," he says.

"We're going from the kind of weather activity which takes out an airport, and which the system kind of copes withâ¦ to a situation where, with very little predictability, it would be possible to lose all of the London airports from real-time operation quite swiftly and with little notice".

Such a scenario, he thinks, would make the disruption for passengers caused by the incident in June seem relatively minor â and could even put lives at risk. But how likely is it?

For controllers at National Air Traffic Services's (NATS) main control centre in Swanwick, Hampshire, thunderstorms are a major preoccupation. When I visit, it is a hot and humid day, there are ominous dark clouds in the sky, and heavy, thundery downpours are hitting the surrounding region.

In Terminal Control, the part of the building responsible for flights heading to or from airports in London and southern England, those same storm cells are being monitored continuously. Their progress is tracked on large radar screens, while at the same time weather forecasts are carefully scrutinised.

Air traffic tends to come in waves during the day. Right now, at lunchtime, it is relatively quiet and the storms have yet to affect major routes. But with activity expected to intensify in the afternoon, staff are already preparing for what could be a tricky few hours.

Stormy weather can shrink the available airspace, meaning traffic has to be restricted and rerouted. If planes are unable to land at one airport, for example due to high winds, they may need diverting to another. But that can create its own problems; if too many unscheduled aircraft land at an already busy airport, it might not have sufficient infrastructure to accommodate them all.

Heavy rain and storms can make flying harder

Planes need fuel and somewhere to park. Passengers need food and facilities. Ultimately, if there isn't space, it can simply refuse to accept all but emergency traffic.

This is where the scenario set out by Atkinson comes in. He warns of a situation where traffic is diverted from Heathrow, Gatwick, Luton and Stansted at the same time, leaving aircraft coming in across the Atlantic and a large number of European flights all looking for somewhere to land at the same time, and some with dwindling fuel reserves.

This would at the very least create serious disruption for passengers, who might end up hundreds of miles from their destinations. But it could also, he thinks, create "a very serious risk event, such as an aircraft running out of fuel in the sky".

Aircraft are legally required to carry enough fuel to take them to an alternative airport if necessary, and to allow them to fly for an extra 30 minutes on top of that.

Not everyone shares Atkinson's concern. Former airline executive Andrew Charlton, who manages the Swiss-based consultancy Aviation Advocacy, thinks the risk is low.

"You've always got to imagine the worst-case scenario", he says. "But I also think we need to keep that worst case in context.

"Yes, it's possible to imagine it. But I'm also fairly comfortable with the ability of this industry to react well and make good decisionsâ¦ of all the things that wake me up at 3amâ¦this is not the top of that list."

Thunderstorms are caused by what is known as convective activity, where moist air close to the ground warms rapidly and rises quickly. As it rises, it expands and cools, allowing the moisture it carries to condense, forming large clouds. More heat means more convective energy, potentially creating instability. In addition, for every degree the Earth warms, the atmosphere can hold about 7% more moisture, increasing the probability of heavy rainfall.

It is not clear whether this means we will actually get more thunderstorms in the UK and across Europe, as so many variables are involved beyond the simple matter of energy and moisture. Some parts of the world may even get fewer. However, studies have shown that higher latitudes, particularly in the northern hemisphere, will see the biggest increases in favourable conditions for storms to form, and those that do may be more violent. According to the UK's Met Office, "while we may not see more thunderstorms overall, the ones we do experience could be more impactful".

Where possible, pilots will avoid flying through convective weather. Even though aircraft are designed to cope with severe conditions, storms can produce intense turbulence, capable of damaging the structural frame of the plane, as well as throwing people around the cabin if they are not strapped in. Hail can also be a big problem, potentially cracking windscreens or causing engine problems. So, however much inconvenience it may cause passengers, crews will invariably put safety first.

New technology helps pilots navigate tricky flying conditions

Onboard weather radars can help aircrew to see up to 320 nautical miles ahead. They also have access to live weather forecasting resources online. But thunderstorms can move quickly and, according to Prof Guy Gratton, an expert in aeronautics at Cranfield University, these systems are essentially "tactical rather than strategic". In other words, although they enable pilots to see storm cells on their screens from a relatively short distance away, and request a diversion, they do not allow for detailed long-distance planning.

The result is that unplanned diversions often happen late in flight, which is likely to mean burning more fuel. If storm delays do become more common, says Andrew Charlton, then aircraft will have to load more fuel as a matter of course, in case there is trouble brewing ahead. "Aircraft are going to have to fly further, and they're going to have to factor in the assumption they're going to fly further," he says. For an airline, fuel is one of its major costs â and if the fuel bill goes up, he says, it is inevitable passengers will pay more.

More intense storms would be likely to make air traffic management in busy regions harder and potentially lead to more delays and cancellations. However, it is possible some of this could be mitigated using new technologies.

Both Heathrow and Gatwick, for example, have moved away from mandating minimum distances between incoming aircraft in favour of a system which continually calculates spacing times based on wind speeds and aircraft types. In practice, this is meant to allow controllers to reduce the distance between landing aircraft, particularly in high winds, and get them on the ground more quickly. According to NATS, it has successfully improved landing rates and reduced delays by a significant margin.

However, there could be other effects from more severe storms as well. Heavier rainfall, for instance, increases the likelihood of flooding. In a worst case, this can paralyse airport operations, as happened in April 2024 when torrential downpours hit Dubai International Airport. It briefly had to close its runways, while disruption lasted for two days. More than 1,200 flights were cancelled, affecting tens of thousands of passengers.

Passengers were delayed during the floods in Dubai

But even if runways and taxiways themselves are clear, and aircraft are able to fly without any difficulty, passengers may struggle to catch their flights if roads, railways or car parks are flooded.

Protecting against such risks can be expensive. Gatwick Airport, which experienced severe flooding over the Christmas period in 2013, has spent tens of millions of pounds on mitigation strategies in recent years â although those figures are dwarfed by the more than Â£2bn it is expecting to invest in its current expansion plans.

The problem of clear-air turbulence is also a real one. It cannot be seen, and is becoming increasingly common as the climate warms.

This is when air moves violently at high altitudes with no clouds or other visual cues to help a pilot avoid it. For passengers, it usually only means a little bit of bumpiness, but occasionally it can be a lot more serious. On 1 March 2023, for example, a Lufthansa flight from Austin, Texas to Frankfurt encountered what the airline described as "brief but severe" turbulence.

Those aboard described how passengers and flight crew were thrown around the cabin as the plane suddenly lost altitude. After it made an emergency landing in Washington DC, seven people were taken to hospital.

While still rare, this kind of turbulence is already much more prevalent than it used to be and is likely to become even more so.

"You know the projections are pretty dire: doubling, trebling or worse in the amount of severe turbulence on many busy flight routes around the world," says Paul Williams, a professor of atmospheric science at the University of Reading. He says incidents over the North Atlantic have increased by 55% since 1979.

Nevertheless, he remains confident solutions can be found, including improvements to forecasting and making use of laser-based technology.

There is another climate-related threat to aviation, which may prove challenging to overcome: rising sea levels. When Hurricane Sandy arrived off the US East Coast in October 2012, it provoked a storm surge that flooded the city. The two runways at New York's LaGuardia airport were left under water for several days, preventing it from reopening even after the winds had subsided.

Significant flooding that would have affected the New York City area once in every 500 years in pre-industrial times is now experienced every 25 years or so â and could occur every five years by the middle of the century, according to one major study. That makes LaGuardia, built on reclaimed land next to Flushing Bay, appear highly vulnerable.

LaGuardia could be at greater risk of flooding and future closures

It is not alone. With some 40% of the world's population living within 100km of coastlines, many airports have been built on low-lying areas near the sea. According to research by Newcastle University, 269 airports around the world are already at risk of flooding due to rising sea levels, and that total is expected to rise. Among those potentially most exposed are the likes of Amsterdam Schiphol, London City, Bahrain International and Newark Liberty International.

Many airports do already have some degree of flood protection, especially larger ones. But stopping the flood risks from rising over the next century means expensive investment in flood defences, raising runways, or even moving airports. It could cost up to $57bn (Â£42bn) according to the Newcastle report. Such costs are ultimately likely to be met by taxpayers or by travellers.

The aviation industry is often criticised for its own contribution to climate change emissions. It faces a forbidding challenge in attempting to meet a target of being carbon neutral by 2050, while also accommodating growth that is expected to see the global aircraft fleet double in size over the next two decades.

The overlooked factor driving the childhood obesity crisis

The race to stop England running out of water

How a new era of heatwaves could shake up the school calendar

Yet it is clear the impacts of climate change will also be felt by airlines, airports and inevitably by passengers -

"I think the best we can do is mitigate", says Andrew Charlton.

"We need to make sure we have enough space at air terminals for people whose flights are being disrupted and delayed, that we have enough hotel rooms and parking space and so on."

But ultimately, he says, "disagreeable moments are going to happen more".

Passengers, he thinks, will have to accept it as a fact of flying life.

#237. What Wall Street billionaires are saying about Democrats' midterm sweep

America's billionaires woke up Wednesday to a political reckoning — and a Wall Street rally.

As Democrats celebrated a sweeping midterm victory, some of the country's wealthiest donors traded barbs over record election spending. Others warned the results could threaten the artificial intelligence boom.

Wall Street, however, cheered the outcome. The Dow Jones Industrial Average soared 1,569 points, or 3%, while the S&P 500 gained 1.6% and the Nasdaq Composite rose 1.4%, as investors welcomed political gridlock, reduced uncertainty and President Donald Trump's willingness to accept the results.

Virgin Group founder Richard Branson took aim at the billions spent influencing the election.

"I have felt embarrassed by the billions that tech entrepreneurs and hedge fund managers have spent," Branson wrote Wednesday on LinkedIn, calling for campaign finance reform. Branson did not give donations that met the reporting threshold in the 2026 election cycle.

His comments came as Elon Musk, the election's largest individual donor, watched his preferred Republicans lose the Texas Senate and governor races despite pouring $224 million into the state — nearly half his record $474 million in political giving nationwide, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records.

Early Wednesday, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO suggested on X that data centers in space were looking increasingly appealing before joking that his "election-interfering space lasers" had failed.

LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, another major political donor, took a sharper tone.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote late Tuesday on X, accusing Musk of spending "outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power" while defending his own contributions to Democrats.

Other Democratic megadonors celebrated. "The world changed yesterday," Alex Soros wrote Wednesday on X, hailing the results as a generational victory after his family contributed roughly $103 million to Democratic political efforts.

But beyond the political sparring, some of the country's richest investors were focused on a potentially bigger consequence of the Democratic sweep: the future of the AI boom.

Even before polls closed Tuesday, Pershing Square founder Bill Ackman warned on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that slowing AI development could jeopardize America's technological edge. "Practically speaking, we can't slow down," he said.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, billionaire investor Chamath Palihapitiya sounded an even starker warning.

Palihapitiya initially questioned whether the early results would hold, but warned that if they did, the implications for the AI trade could be severe.

"And the only way to sink the AI trade is to sink the data centers," Palihapitiya said during an election night livestream of the "All-In" podcast.

Texas offered an early test of those fears. Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, who has proposed a moratorium on new data center construction until stronger safeguards are established, defeated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

During the same livestream, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner warned the Democratic wave could mean at least 10 to 15 gigawatts of expected AI data center capacity would not come online in 2027. Wall Street analyst Dan Ives estimated Wednesday that 10% of planned U.S. data centers would no longer go ahead.

Advanced Micro Devices CEO Lisa Su acknowledged the growing resistance to AI's expansion, urging technology companies to demonstrate the benefits of data centers to local communities.

"At the end of the day, what we need to do is ensure that everybody wins from AI," Su told CNBC on Wednesday.

Yet Wall Street may have found opportunity in the same slowdown that worried AI's biggest backers.

Fewer new data centers could increase the value of existing facilities while easing the enormous spending burden on technology giants. Big Tech stocks surged Wednesday, adding more than $750 billion in market value at one point, even as smaller, debt-dependent data center operators tumbled.
