#148. Australia's oldest department store David Jones folds after 188 years

Australia's oldest department store, David Jones, has gone into voluntary administration

David Jones, Australia's oldest department store chain, has gone into voluntary administration, putting thousands of jobs at risk.

It comes after years of financial struggles for the 188-year-old retailer - known locally as DJs - as customers desert traditional shops for online shopping.

David Jones's physical and online stores will continue to operate while receivers FTI Consulting review the company, said voluntary administrators McGrathNicol.

"David Jones is an iconic company and so many Australians have stories and memories of visiting DJ's stores," said its CEO Erica Berthold, adding the brand has a "well-developed plan" to "reset the business for the future".

Berthold thanked "customers, our team members and our partners for their continued support of David Jones through this period".

David Jones employs about 6,000 staff and has 38 physical stores, with 17 of those in Westfield shopping centres.

Hours before the board voted to go into administration, Scentre - which owns Westfield - said the retailer had not paid its rent for October and was "therefore in breach" of its tenancy agreement.

"We understand the current retail trading environment, rising costs, a significant and outdated retail footprint, and supply chain challenges have led to this point," said Christopher Hill from receivers FTI Consulting.

By appointing administrators and receivers, David Jones will get "breathing space" and a chance to restructure the business, Hill said.

For many Australians, David Jones - and its trademark black-and-white houndstooth design - represented luxury.

But its collapse signalled the "death knell" for the department store format, said Jana Bowden, who teaches marketing at Macquarie University's Business School.

"It was once the epitome of glamour shopping in Australia - the Aussie version of Harrods," she said, "a place you would go to see and be a part of the glitz and glamour, where you could marvel at all the premium luxury labels, and feel like you'd made it."

Visiting the flagship city stores in Sydney and Melbourne was also a ritual for many Australian families, especially to see the festive Christmas window displays.

"People would travel from hours away from the city just to see those windows and experience the magic," Bowden said, but in recent years, the brand had lost its shine.

"The city stores have retained their upper end airs and graces, but the suburban stores leave a lot to be desired â they're mundane, they lack excitement and there's definitely no glitz," she said.

During Queen Elizabeth II's first visit to Sydney in 1954, the then-27-year-old monarch attended a banquet dinner on the seventh floor of the Elizabeth St store, according to the Sydney Morning Herald.

#147. CVS Health plans to split itself up. Here's why a breakup could make sense now

CVS Health plans to break itself up — and this time, the bet looks a lot more viable.

The healthcare conglomerate on Wednesday announced plans to split into two publicly traded companies by the first half of 2028. CVS would separate its pharmacy business – including roughly 9,000 retail locations – from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark.

The move comes more than two years after CVS considered a breakup but ultimately walked away from the idea. At the time, analysts questioned whether the retail pharmacy business could survive on its own.

“Back then, the idea of retail standing alone was unthinkable,” Brian Tanquilut, senior equity research analyst at Jefferies, told CNBC.

But the economics of the businesses have changed: CVS's retail pharmacy business has become more consistently profitable, while its insurer, Aetna, has improved its performance despite persistently high medical costs in Medicare Advantage plans. That could make it easier for the company to separate the units — and for investors to recognize the value of each on its own, rather than valuing them together as one sprawling healthcare conglomerate.

“For us, it's showing the stability of the pharmacy,” Michael Cherny, senior research analyst at Leerink Partners, said in an interview. “This business is a much different business from the one that's been considered three years ago, five years ago, 10 years ago, where there had been a litany of headwinds that the pharmacy just couldn't overcome.”

CVS CEO David Joyner told CNBC’s "Squawk on the Street" on Wednesday that back in 2024, the company was "in a turnaround" without the flexibility, cash or business performance to execute a split. Now, CVS has posted eight consecutive quarters of beating Wall Street earnings estimates and has raised its guidance several times over the last two years, thanks to improvement across its PBM, pharmacy and insurer.

Since taking the helm in 2024, Joyner has pursued an aggressive turnaround plan that involves $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shakeups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

The company also now expects to generate at least $15.5 billion in operating cash flow in 2026, a roughly 70% increase from $9.1 billion in 2024, and says it has reached its leverage target ahead of schedule.

"What's changed in two years is the fact that we now can look at this business and the assets that are assembled and really determine, without any distress or without anything that we have to do, what is the right strategic decision to make," Joyner told analysts on a call Wednesday, adding that the decision comes "out of strength."

While investors have largely cheered CVS's progress in recent years, Wall Street appeared more pessimistic about Wednesday's announcement, as the company's shares dropped about 12%.

CVS's retail business has benefited from changes in the competitive landscape, including the widespread retrenchment and store closures of Walgreens, Rite Aid and other pharmacy operators over the last few years. That’s created opportunities for CVS to gain market share, some analysts told CNBC.

CVS has also introduced changes to its pharmacy reimbursement model. Its CostVantage program is designed to replace the traditional process with a more transparent, cost-based model. Some analysts say that has helped the company better align reimbursement with the cost of dispensing prescriptions and contributed to steadier profits.

Those developments represent a meaningful shift for a business that has faced years of pressure from declining prescription reimbursement, store closures and competition from online pharmacies and other retailers.

That doesn't mean the retail pharmacy has become a high-growth business. CVS's retail pharmacy segment remains a relatively low-margin operation, with adjusted operating margins of roughly 4% to 5%. But analysts say it may now be stable and profitable enough to operate independently – even if investors value it at a discount to CVS's other businesses.

In a note Wednesday, RBC Capital Markets analyst Ben Hendrix said the lower valuation investors assign to the retail pharmacy unit has weighed on CVS's overall value and obscured the earnings strength of businesses like Aetna and Caremark.

The underlying growth of the insurance and PBM businesses is also stronger than that of retail pharmacy, Tanquilut said, after accounting for changes to Caremark's rebate arrangements.

Aetna in particular has gotten a better handle on medical costs by exiting unprofitable markets, expanding value-based care models, and tightening utilization management.

The breakup also comes as Caremark faces mounting scrutiny over PBM practices, including state-level legal and regulatory challenges involving the businesses and their relationships with pharmacies.

Republicans in Tennessee and Arkansas have passed laws that would ban any one company from owning both a pharmacy and a PBM — thought the Arkansas measure has been held up in court. Cherny noted that legal hurdles involving PBMs in other states have continued to pile up.

“Through what seems to be a never-ending litany of state lawsuits, it feels like it's been under more pressure,” Cherny said. “It's unclear if it just became, for lack of a better term, too tenuous to move forward with those two entities combined as one. I’m not sure.”

Even so, Joyner told the Wall Street Journal that the company wasn’t splitting up because of regulatory issues. CVS has said the separation follows a strategic review and is intended to give each business greater focus and flexibility.

The move also marks a significant departure from the integrated healthcare strategy CVS spent years building.

CVS spent roughly $70 billion to acquire Aetna in 2018 and argued that combining health insurance, pharmacy benefit management, retail pharmacies and health providers under one roof would help coordinate care and create a competitive advantage. CVS was still promoting that model as recently as its investor day last year.

The proposed split suggests the company now sees more value in separating at least some of those businesses – though it is not abandoning vertical integration altogether.

Aetna and Caremark will remain together in the healthcare company, alongside CVS’s specialty pharmacy, its primary care clinic Oak Street Health and its home-visit provider Signify Health. The separate pharmacy company will include roughly 9,000 retail stores, mail pharmacy operations and MinuteClinic, a retail medical clinic located inside certain CVS locations.

Both sides are large enough that they can stand on their own, Cherny said. Caremark is one of the country's three largest PBMs, while CVS has one of the largest retail pharmacy footprints in the U.S.

But separating the company raises questions about how much of the commercial relationship between the businesses can survive.

The relationship has historically benefited both sides. As it stands, Caremark can use CVS's extensive retail footprint to offer members convenient access to prescriptions and steer them toward CVS locations through preferred pharmacy arrangements. Programs such as Maintenance Choice, for example, give eligible members the option to fill certain maintenance medications at CVS retail pharmacies or through mail order.

Those arrangements also helped direct prescription volume to CVS's stores, giving the retail pharmacy unit an important source of customers. Because the businesses shared a parent company, the financial benefits of that relationship ultimately went to CVS as a whole.

In a note, RBC's Hendrix also raised questions about how the split could impact Aetna's Medicare Advantage quality ratings, which determine whether health plans qualify for bonus payments from the government. Retail pharmacists have played a role in engaging Aetna members, and a separation could complicate that relationship.

But Joyner told analysts Wednesday that the synergy between the two businesses would be "preserved in commercial agreements between the two organizations," adding that "we don't need the integration of an operating structure."

Joyner said CVS will be working on those potential agreements over the next 15 to 18 months.

Preferred pharmacy networks are common across the industry, and separate ownership does not prevent Caremark from continuing to favor CVS pharmacies through negotiated arrangements, Cherny said. The difference is that under separate ownership, the profits from those arrangements would no longer accrue to a single company, he noted.

“The issue is not whether the companies can continue working together in some capacity, but whether any future contractual arrangements preserve the SAME competitive advantages and flexibility as today under common ownership,” Cherny said in an afternoon note on Wednesday.

Still, both businesses face challenges on their own.

Tanquilut cautioned that some of CVS's recent retail pharmacy gains have come as Walgreens and other competitors have closed stores, raising questions about whether the growth can be sustained. The business also remains exposed to consumer pressure and competition from online pharmacies.

Caremark, meanwhile, faces continued regulatory scrutiny and potential headwinds from changes to the federal 340B drug discount program, which requires drugmakers to offer discounted medications to eligible hospitals and other healthcare providers serving low-income and vulnerable patients.

CVS participates in the program by providing pharmacy services to those organizations, but tweaks to manufacturer data-sharing policies and potential reimbursement changes could weigh on that business in 2027, Tanquilut said.

Aetna, meanwhile, is still working through a multiyear plan to rebuild its margins, as it looks to improve the profitability of its Medicare Advantage business.

Ultimately, CVS is betting that the value of letting investors assess its businesses separately will outweigh the benefits of keeping them under one roof. The split will test whether each can thrive independently.

#146. Jim Cramer says the AI sell-off isn't over, but it isn't another dot-com crash

The steep sell-off in artificial intelligence stocks likely has further to go, CNBC's Jim Cramer predicted Thursday — but he urged investors not to panic.

Growing opposition to new data centers following the midterm elections has raised doubts about future AI infrastructure spending, sending shares of companies tied to the buildout sharply lower. Micron tumbled 17%, GE Vernova dropped 7% and Sandisk plunged 22% on Thursday. All three stocks were also crushed in Wednesday's session.

"I wish I could say that today's flush was the bottom," the "Mad Money" host said, before cautioning that there may be more downside ahead, though he didn't believe this was an internet bubble redux. Cramer argued that today's AI companies are fundamentally different from the speculative businesses that collapsed during the dot-com bust.

"But I look at the charts, and there still seems like there's no support for so many Tech stocks, especially the semis, because they ran so much on the way up. However it might feel, it's actually an orderly decline because unlike 2000, these are mostly lucrative companies with stocks that went too high."

Investors had pushed AI stocks too high by assuming the rapid pace of data center construction would continue indefinitely, Cramer said. Smaller developers such as CoreWeave, Iren and Nebius face particular risks because they borrowed money to finance projects that could now face regulatory delays.

"That's what I am chiefly worried about," he said. "That's the real problem. That's what could really hurt the stock market. ... Right now, no one of significance is defaulting on their debt. The only issue with the debt is that it's connected to those neocloud companies that I mentioned. That's not enough to sink us."

Cramer suggested that a swift sell-off could help the market work off the excesses of the AI boom more effectively than a prolonged downturn.

"I for one am glad they are ripping the Band-Aid off this market rather than slowing trying to take it off a little bit each day," he said.

For investors, Cramer recommended maintaining sufficient cash. While semiconductor stocks could face further declines, he pointed to falling interest rates, signs of easing inflation and the possibility of an end to the Iran war as reasons for optimism.

"If you have cash you won't panic," Cramer concluded. "You won't be complacent, either. You will be clear-headed and that's how you will lose less money until things get better. They always do."

#145. UK and EU agree draft deal to scrap Brexit red tape over food exports

The UK and EU have agreed a draft deal on removing post-Brexit checks from food and agriculture exports, ahead of the UK-EU summit later this month.

The BBC understands the 200-page draft text for this so called "SPS" [sanitary and phytosanitary] agreement has been circulated to EU nations in Brussels for consideration at a meeting on Wednesday.

Similar texts for deals on emissions trading and a youth travel and work scheme have also been circulated alongside a 15-page draft joint statement for UK and EU top leaders.

The food deal is the most significant part of the so-called Brexit reset agreed so far, removing checks and red tape that hit exporters after the UK exited the EU's single market.

As a result of the agreement, the government claims the UK economy will eventually see Â£5.1bn worth of annual benefits.

Speaking during the Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting (Chogm) in Antigua and Barbuda, the prime minister said a UK-EU deal could add up to 0.4% extra on GDP.

The deal could have the following impact:

Exports to the EU of fresh sausages, burgers, shellfish from domestic waters and seed potatoes would be able to resume

It should remove the need for Â£200 Export Health Certificates, which confirm the product meets the health requirements of the country it is heading to

It should also end the need for Â£25 Phytosanitary certificates, which show that plants or plant products are free from pests and diseases

Inspector fees, organic certificates, some port fees and other checks for exports and imports to and from the EU could be removed

It will bring back passports for British pets being taken into the EU

In return, the UK is expected to commit to observing and adapting to existing EU regulations on plant, animal and food trade standards - a process called "dynamic alignment".

Each of the elements on the table was up for discussion under Sir Keir Starmer, and the summit would have taken place by now had Labour not replaced its leader in July.

Under Andy Burnham, Britain's approach to post-Brexit relations with the EU have shifted significantly. Sir Keir said the UK would not rejoin the bloc in his lifetime, while Burnham has said he would like to see Britain back in the EU in his.

At Chogm on Tuesday, Burnham said: "I think people in Britain would expect me to take that with both hands, given where we've been in recent times, and that's what I'm doing."

Pressed on whether it would be acceptable for the UK to have its rules on food trade made by the EU, he said: "We're not in the EU, therefore we do have to work with them in a different way.

"But if that can get growth, if that can help our farmers, if that can remove red tape from British businesses, I think most people would expect me to go to that summit and negotiate the best deal that I can.

"And that's what I've been doing in recent weeks, leaning in, being positive, having a really constructive relationship with our European partners and hopefully we can land a good deal for Britain in terms of growth but also I hope more collaboration on controlling immigration."

Jon Walsh of Bio&Me said his firm had stopped exporting yoghurt due to vets' fees

Jon Walsh, the chief executive and co-founder of Bio&Me, which makes granolas, mueslis and yoghurts, told BBC 5 Live the draft deal sounded like "really good news".

"We're just a small company but when we were trying to export our yoghurts before, every time we'd send a pallet we had to pay a vet to inspect it and that was Â£140, and that meant that you'd lose money... so we had to stop."

The company also had problems exporting granola and overnight oats, with some shipments being returned.

"It was so frustrating because it was the same person filling out the same form and yet half would make it through and half wouldn't," Walsh said.

"You just kind of give up because you're losing money."

In an interview with ITV, Burnham said "public opinion has changed nationally" since the 2016 Brexit vote, but added he would "not run ahead of public opinion" on the issue.

"The public need to be the boss when it comes to whatever happens with Brexit," he added.

Burnham also said he had not proposed the return of freedom of movement between the UK and EU.

Asked by the BBC about the UK's relationship with the EU, Tory Leader Kemi Badenoch said that while we should "work closely", "dynamic alignment" with the EU would be a red line and "actually take us backwards".

Liberal Democrat Europe spokesperson Al Pinkerton said the draft deal was a "patch, not a fix".

"The real solution is to rejoin the single market and to establish a customs union with the EU," he added.

Reform UK's business spokesman Richard Tice said: "One of the key benefits of Brexit was that Britain no longer had to adhere to ridiculous EU rules.

"Returning to any form of alignment with Europe would crush Britain's competitiveness and force us to surrender sovereignty over trade policy, putting trade deals worth hundreds of billions of pounds at risk."

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen with Prime Minister Andy Burnham

While UK rules remain mostly aligned with EU ones, there are some areas where England has diverged from the EU rule book, for example on "precision breeding" of plants, previously known as gene editing. In recent weeks, agreement has been struck on transition arrangements while the EU's new regulations come in to force.

The government has already announced a European Partnerships Bill which will provide a legal mechanism for the application of new EU rules and regulations. This will also allow for the necessary alignments for the linking of the UK emissions trading scheme with the EU's, and save the need for UK exporters to pay the EU's new carbon border tax.

The draft agreements will be discussed at a meeting of EU ambassadors called Coreper on Wednesday, with some key details on the planned numbers in a Youth Experience Scheme to be finalised at a political level in the coming days. A discount on full foreign student fees for EU students is likely to be pushed to next year alongside a plan to reconnect electricity markets.

The BBC understands the draft agreements will also include some references to UK involvement in defence co-operation schemes, access to law enforcement databases and some reassurances over the EU's "Made in Europe" plans.

A food deal will have particular significance for Northern Ireland, radically reducing the impact of the post-Brexit Irish Sea border.

Northern Ireland's Brexit deal kept it inside the EU's single market for goods. That means food products from elsewhere in the UK require paperwork and some physical checks when arriving at NI's ports. A deal will ultimately end those requirements.

While the food trade plans have been backed by supermarkets and farmers representatives, some pro-Brexit campaigners have criticised the reapplication of EU rules. The post-Brexit trade deal signed by Boris Johnson's government prioritised regulatory freedoms for the UK over avoiding new regulatory red tape, checks and delays.

But William Bain, the British Chamber of Commerce's (BCC) head of trade policy, told the BBC's Today programme that business overall thought it was a "price is worth paying" to follow common rules, as companies would benefit from "lower costs, reduced delays [and] increased market access".

At the moment, he said, the requirement for Export Health Certificates can add thousands of pounds of costs to each truck carrying goods, in addition "to the delays that people find at the ports or at the borders".

"So we get lower exports because of the delays and the massive increases in costs that have come into the supply chain."

The BCC has released a new analysis of its members' views suggesting options for a closer relationship.

The business lobby group says the current trade relationship is "failing to deliver", leaving firms "struggling with a thicket of Brexit red tape" nearly six years after the UK's exit from the bloc.

#144. From tanks to drones: South Korea’s defense firms face a new battlefield test

South Korean defense stocks rode a wave of global rearmament over the past two years.

Russia’s invasion of Ukraine accelerated military spending across Europe. South Korean companies drew buyers with weapons that were competitively priced and could be delivered relatively quickly, including tanks, artillery and missile systems.

But that rally has given way to a sharp sell-off, with shares of several major South Korean defense companies plunging this year.

The combined market value of the top four Korean defense companies — Hanwha Aerospace, LIG D&A, Hyundai Rotem, and Korea Aerospace — had plunged to about 62 trillion won ($46.18 billion) as of end-October from their 52-week peak of 170 trillion won, according to South Korean broadcaster SBS.

Are South Korean companies, known for exporting tanks and artillery systems, well-placed for a modern battlefield that has seen drones and counter-drone systems proliferate in Ukraine and Iran?

The war in Ukraine has shown how relatively inexpensive drones can threaten expensive military equipment, and drone strikes from Iran have depleted stocks of costly air defense missiles of the U.S. and its Gulf allies.

"Modern warfare is being fought with less traditional weaponry (tanks, cannons, armored vehicles, etc.), which Koreans are positioned for. Even on this front, drones, for example, are dominated by China, pricing out other potential competitors from Europe, the U.S., and Asia," said Peter Kim, senior managing director at KB Securities.

KJ Hwang, Korea autos & industrials analyst, at Bank of America agreed that procurement priorities were evolving, with more focus on drones, counter-drone systems and air defense.

The reversal in defense stocks is not all down to new warfighting platforms.

The declines come as investors weigh several concerns: disappointing earnings, a stronger won and delayed export orders.

Fund flows into the semiconductor sector and high valuations built on expectations of strong earnings growth have also weighed on Korean defense shares, said KB Securities' Kim, adding that governments have moved slower than expected to convert defense spending pledges into approved budgets and orders.

Financial results have amplified those concerns. Hanwha Aerospace’s third-quarter operating profit fell over 46% from a year earlier, with the company citing delivery-schedule adjustments and one-off costs. Its shares are down more than 28% so far this year.

Hyundai Rotem, which reported a more than 11.1% decline in operating profit, has seen its stock drop almost 60%.

Korea Aerospace Industries’ third-quarter sales and operating profit improved about 52% and 32% respectively, limiting the decline in its shares to just over 8% this year.

Experts, however, are still positive on the sector, especially over the long-term.

"South Korean defense companies have continued to expand cooperation with partners in Western Europe and the Middle East, including in air-defense systems, while additional K9 howitzer orders suggest that supply constraints and the continued importance of deterrence remain supportive of demand for conventional platforms," BofA's Hwang said.

Some deals made this year include K9 orders to Spain from Hanwha Aerospace, valued at about 546 million euros, as well as a $922 million order for K239 rocket systems from Norway.

LIG D&A has made a $94 million deal with Malaysia for its K-SAAM naval surface-to-air guided missile system, the first export deal of the system ever made.

Countries still rely heavily on conventional weapons to bolster their defense capabilities, with KB Securities' Kim saying that the Korean defense sector "remains attractive, given the long-term growth outlook is still in the teens."

David Roche, strategist at Quantum Strategy, told CNBC that there is a "greater emphasis" on AI and drone technology in military orders as major powers learn the lessons of Ukraine, however, “drones cannot capture and hold terrain ... So, in any mobile battlefield, infantry, tanks, armor etc, are essential."

South Korean defense companies are also actively investing in drones and unmanned systems, and while meaningful exports will take time as overseas buyers require a proven operational track record, Kim expects more tangible results potentially emerging in the near future.

#143. Kellogg's admits snap, crackle and pop is quieter after uproar over Rice Krispies recipe change

Kellogg's has told the BBC the "snap, crackle and pop" sound made by its Rice Krispies may be noticeably quieter after an uproar over the cereal giant's recipe change.

Rice Krispies and Coco Pops are no longer being made with whole rice grains, with the tiny toasted puffs being made using corn flour and rice flour instead.

A Kellogg's spokesperson said retaining the iconic sound had been a "huge focus" when designing the new recipe, but that customers "might notice that the sound may be quieter and not as instant as before".

The firm was responding to complaints online that the taste of the breakfast staples had changed, with some fans adding they missed the "pop" sound.

Kellogg's said it had adapted the recipes due to "changing weather conditions" that have disrupted supplies of some grains and cereals.

The cereal giant said the new versions had been "carefully assessed for taste, appearance and texture", but some shoppers said their children would no longer eat them.

Some expressed dismay that the taste and texture were "totally different" and dubbed the change "grim" and "disgusting".

"There's no snap, crackle and pop in these," a parent named Dani wrote on TikTok. "My toddler won't eat them."

Lauren, also posting on TikTok, added that she had been eating Rice Krispies every day for breakfast "for years and years".

"I had a bowl of the new recipe and you can immediately tell the difference," she said. "They don't have the snapping sound when you put in the milk."

Jessicake meanwhile said they tasted "weird and stale".

The "Snap, Crackle and Pop" - made when milk is added to the puffed rice - has been a centrepiece of the brand's marketing since the 1950s.

Kellogg's insisted the snap, crackle and pop is "still there, but consumers might notice that the sound may be quieter and not as instant as before".

Recipe changes are notoriously difficult for big brands. Chocolate makers are constantly accused of reducing cocoa content in their products and debates rage online over whether ketchup ingredients have changed.

When Scottish fizzy drink Irn Bru cut its sugar content in 2018, fans famously stockpiled crates of the old version.

No changes are being made to Rice Krispies products in the US and Canada, which are owned by a separate company.

Kellogg's said the recipe change in the UK would help "maintain consistent quality" as extreme weather takes a toll on crops and pushes up ingredient prices.

Extreme weather in Spain in 2022 and 2023 in particular had reduced availability of the types of rice the company uses for its breakfast cereals, the company said.

Rice Krispies, launched nearly a century ago, are sold around the world and come in variations such as vanilla and strawberry flavours in some markets alongside the widespread chocolate-flavoured Coco Pops version.

"We appreciate that even small changes to a much-loved product can feel noticeable and that's why we have approached this with a lot of care and consideration," Kellogg's said.

The firm said it appreciated the customer feedback and would "continue to look at ways to make [its] products even better in the future".

The cereals have already undergone some change, with reductions in salt content in the 1990s and lowered sugar in 2018. The company said Rice Krispies and Coco Pops will continue to meet UK food standards on levels of fat, salt and sugar in food.

A backlash against processed foods in recent years, especially those low in fibre and high in sugar, has put breakfast cereals generally under the spotlight from healthy eating advocates.

Kellogg's stressed the cereals contain no artificial colours, flavours or sweeteners and the nutritional benefits in each bowl, such as fibre, Vitamin D and iron are staying the same.

However, the new recipes mean packaging will now include a "contains wheat gluten" warning. It also continues to contain barley-malt extract.

Watch: How does El NinÌo affect world weather?

Kellogg's said it has not changed the recipe for any of its other cereals.

Many parts of the world have been affected by droughts, floods and heatwaves this year due to the El NiÃ±o weather phenomenon. The weather event is expected to severely affect crop production from South East Asia in particular. The region produces more than a quarter of the world's rice.

The World Meteorological Agency expects the record-breaking El NiÃ±o will last until at least 2027.

That will intensify pressure on food manufacturing companies, already looking at ways to source reliable and affordable ingredients.

The original Kellogg company separated into two distinct firms in 2023.

The largest is Kellanova, which focuses on snacks, cereals, noodles and other food products like Eggo, Pringles and Pop-Tarts sold globally. It is now owned by Mars, which makes confectionery and pet food.

The other business, WK Kellogg's, operates in North America and has since been taken over by Italian chocolate giant Ferrero.

#141. Elon Musk's millions failed to deliver Republican wins in fierce midterms

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

Elon Musk spent almost $450m (Â£334m) â more than anyone ever has in an election cycle â to back Republican political candidates in the US midterm races. It was not enough to keep Democrats from a resounding victory.

Among the most striking wins went to Democrat James Talarico, who beat Republican Ken Paxton for a US Senate seat in Texas. Musk spent at least $45m in support of Paxton, according to a BBC analysis of campaign finance data.

Other races where Musk spent millions included Arizona, Michigan, Wisconsin and Minnesota. His spending continued into Monday, records show.

By Tuesday night, Musk seemed to admit defeat, joking on X that his "election-interfering space lasers didn't work."

LIVE UPDATES: All the latest developments

BOWEN: Trump's loss limits powers at home, but it will likely be different abroad

RESULTS: How America voted in maps and charts

EXPLAINER: Five reasons why Trump did so badly

ZURCHER: Midterm carnage weakens Trump but he still wields power for now

REACTION: Angry Republicans blame Trump for midterms 'disaster'

NEWSLETTER: Sign up for Anthony Zurcher's analysis in your inbox

The loss this week of so many Republican seats may have significant implications for Musk and his businesses.

SpaceX has gained an estimated, external $10bn in new government contracts since Trump was elected to his second non-consecutive term. Several Democratic lawmakers have said the company's extensive and growing role in government and national security should be investigated, external.

SpaceX's future development of massive data centres is also facing new potential difficulties - as are the data centre developments of other tech companies.

In Georgia for instance, where SpaceX has data centre operations, the just elected Democratic governor ran in-part on limiting the rapid rise of the massive buildings, as well as the huge tax breaks tech companies usually get to build them.

A number of other politicians ran on limiting data centres and dozens of state measures to limit their development passed on Tuesday, external.

Data centres are sprawling buildings that house stacks of computing hardware. Tech and artificial intelligence (AI) companies use them to run online platforms such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Musk's X and his AI company xAI.

Musk posted to X on Tuesday that his previously floated idea to somehow build data centres in space was "looking pretty appealing right now."

Five reasons why Republicans lost so badly

Democrats won Congress in a blue wave. Now comes the hard part.

Tech companies involved in AI may be in for a tougher government scrutiny and potential regulation with Democrats in power, though any legislation would need Trump's approval.

James Flintoft, head of investment solutions at AJ Bell, pointed to Musk's unsuccessful spending on Republican candidates and the scale of wins by Democrats as bringing in a "period of uncertainty for investors as they assess whether tech companies are set to face greater regulation."

Senator Ed Markey on Wednesday responded on X to a person asking if major tech industry executives would be the target of investigations, saying: "They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing."

Tech stocks were broadly up on Wednesday morning. Yet, Musk's Tesla and SpaceX were down by 4.9% and 3.4% respectively.

By Wednesday, Musk had stopped posting to X about politics and was instead posting in praise of GrokBot, an AI chatbot produced by xAI. It was a stark contrast to the 2025 election, when Musk would post to X sometimes hundreds of times in a day about politics.

He also unpinned a post that had been saved to the top of his X profile since last week that explained his "concern" with Democratic politicians winning more midterm races.

Those concerns included the idea that the US could welcome more immigrants, make Washington DC and Puerto Rico individual states, and expand the number of justices on the Supreme Court.

Another post telling his 241 million X followers to "Vote red today!" appeared on Wednesday to have been deleted from Musk's profile.

The BBC has contacted SpaceX for comment about Musk's spending and posts.

While Musk spent millions of dollars backing Republican candidates in multiple US states, a lot of his political focus seemed to be on Texas.

In recent years, Musk made the state the corporate headquarters of Tesla and SpaceX, both of which still have large operations in California where they were founded.

"There is something deeply wrong with Talarico", Musk posted to X in October.

Talarico posted in response: "There is something deeply wrong with a political system that allows one trillionaire to buy his own personal US Senator."

Not all billionaires in the US shared Musk's preference for Republicans.

Reid Hoffman, a tech billionaire and the founder of LinkedIn who is a major donor to Democrats, hailed the results of Tuesday's elections as an event that "blew the doors off our expectations."

"These elections were an overwhelming wave against Trump and his cronies," Hoffman wrote on X and on LinkedIn. "While people like Elon are spending outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power, those of us who believe in the ideals of America cannot yield."

#138. E.l.f. Beauty posts 51% sales increase, raises full-year guidance

E.l.f. Beauty on Wednesday blew past Wall Street expectations as it posted a 51% increase in net sales in its second fiscal quarter.

The beauty company said it saw its 31st consecutive quarter of net sales growth, attributing the streak to strong performance across its portfolio. The revenue spike was driven by both retail and e-commerce channels across the U.S. and internationally, the company added.

"The thing I'm most pleased about is we saw strength across our entire portfolio," CEO Tarang Amin told CNBC in an interview. "Every one of our brands contributed strong growth this quarter."

Amin added that E.l.f. is the most-searched brand on TikTok Shop and the No. 2 brand in cosmetics on the platform. The company also said it saw strong results for Rhode, the skincare brand it acquired last year.

The company raised its full fiscal-year guidance following the better-than-anticipated quarter. It now expects net sales in the range of $2 billion to $2.03 billion, compared with a previous range of between $1.94 billion and $1.97 billion. It also upped its adjusted EBITDA projection to a range of $421 million to $427 million from between $401 million and $407 million.

E.l.f. said it now expects adjusted earnings per share for the year to come in between $3.82 and $3.86, exceeding prior guidance of between $3.50 and $3.55 per share. It's also expecting a net income range of $231 million to $233 million, up from a previous range of $212 million to $215 million.

It had already raised its guidance last quarter after outperforming its expectations.

Amin said the company is "very bullish" on the holiday season, expecting high sales through the end of the year.

Here's how the company performed in its second fiscal quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the period ended Sept. 30, E.l.f. reported net income of $30.03 million, or 50 cents per share, up significantly from roughly $3 million, or 5 cents per share, the year prior.

E.l.f. also saw significant growth in its international channels, with business up 165%. Amin told CNBC that only 25% of the company's business is outside of the U.S. and that it has a "tremendous opportunity" to build on that momentum.

The company also said its gross margin increased 1.6% during the quarter, including a 0.3% benefit from tariff refunds. Amin previously said the retailer planned to fully reinvest that money into pricing and marketing to continue driving growth.

Though E.l.f. received most of its refunds in its first fiscal quarter, some of those funds flowed through the second quarter, and the company expects to receive a yearly total of around $55 million. Amin told CNBC on Wednesday that the company lowered the price of about 10% of its units by $1 because of the tariff benefits.

"We're seeing great unit momentum based on that," he said.

#137. Food, fuel and taxes – can the Democrats actually make life cheaper?

'Hopefully things get done': Americans react to historic political shift

Two business owners, half a country apart, want the same thing for different reasons.

In Texas, Kim Hanks, who owns Tillies, a hotel, restaurant and spa business near Austin, says rising grocery and fuel costs are forcing her to raise wages for her 300 staff.

"It's been such a moving target for us on affordability," she told the BBC World Service.

In Indiana, Danny Reynolds, who runs Stephenson's of Elkhart, a clothing and bridalwear store, simply wants Washington to get its act together.

"My hope is that we can just find some consistency, some working together," he said.

"We ran on affordability, we won on affordability, and now it's our job to govern on affordability," said House Democratic leader Hakeem Jeffries on MS Now's Morning Joe.

But here comes the difficult part. Democrats will hold Congress. Donald Trump still has the power to block their plans.

House Democrat leader Hakeem Jeffries said "now it's our job to govern on affordability"

The scale of the win was bigger than most forecasters expected, with at least seven Republican-held Senate seats changing hands.

Behind the result was a striking mood: just 21% of Americans are satisfied with the direction of the country, even though the economy looks healthy on paper.

Voters weren't looking at the statistics. They were looking at their grocery bills, electricity bills and the price at the petrol pump.

In rural Texas, rising costs were even hurting Republicans in their own backyard. Sid Miller, the outgoing Republican agriculture commissioner, told me ranchers are getting $300 to $500 less for each head of cattle, while also facing record fertiliser costs.

This was more than a blue wave. It was a vote about the cost of living. So what can Democrats actually do about it?

The outgoing Republican agriculture commissioner for Texas Sid Miller says ranchers 'feel betrayed'

Under the banner "Fighting for an Affordable America," Democrats say healthcare is their first priority.

They want to bring back the extra financial help that made health insurance cheaper under the Affordable Care Act and reverse the cuts to Medicaid signed by President Trump in 2025.

Next come tariffs and energy. Democrats want to scrap the president's import taxes, which they say add thousands of dollars a year to the cost of groceries, fertiliser and building materials.

They also want to end the war in Iran, which they link to petrol prices averaging $4.18 a gallon, marking an increase of nearly 40%.

Housing is next. Some Democrats want to build four million new homes over the next decade.

On tax, they want to make billionaires pay more and increase tax credits for families with children.

It's a long list. But how much of it can they actually deliver?

Investor Ken Fisher, founder of Fisher Investments, puts it bluntly:

"Congress can't get anything past a presidential veto. President Trump can't get anything he wants."

The result, he says, is likely to be two years of relative quiet on the legislative and regulatory front.

In other words, winning in Congress does not give Democrats a free hand. President Trump can still block legislation, and getting around a presidential veto requires a two-thirds majority in both the House and Senate.

Import taxes now account for more than 5% of federal revenue

For voters hoping for lower bills, that may be a disappointing result. They didn't vote for political deadlock.

Take tariffs. Trump is unlikely to abandon a trade policy that is central to his agenda. Democrats will need Republican support if they want to change it.

Bernard Yaros, deputy chief US economist at Oxford Economics, expects the average tariff rate on imports to stay around 10% for the rest of Trump's term.

Tariffs are bringing in more money for the government. Import taxes accounted for just over 1% of federal revenue in 2024. This year, they account for more than 5%.

That growing contribution to government income, Yaros argues, will make tariffs "tough for a future administration to fully dismantle."

What Democrats can and can't do with their new power after taking Congress

President Trump told CNBC he could work with Democrats on "many more things than people understand".

Yaros sees the possibility of a big political trade-off: Democrats agree to extend Republican tax cuts in return for restoring health insurance subsidies. But he doesn't expect a deal until late 2027, with the economic benefits arriving in 2028. That's a long time to wait for anyone struggling with bills today.

Oregon Democrat Ron Wyden, who is set to chair the Finance Committee, says he will seek to "reassert congressional authority" over trade policy

Democrats could also turn up the pressure on businesses.

Henrietta Treyz of consultants Veda Partners says Democrats will question companies that passed on the cost of tariffs to customers.

After the Supreme Court ruling in February, she says, the question will be: "Did you turn around, take that tariff rebate, and then lower prices for consumers?"

Companies will also be asked: "What did you get in exchange?"

Democrats, she adds, will "pick and choose very carefully."

In the Senate, Ron Wyden, the Oregon Democrat set to chair the Finance Committee, has summed up his approach in six words: "affordability for us, accountability for them."

He says he will use the committee to "reassert congressional authority" over trade policy. Insurers, drugmakers and banks should expect to be called to explain themselves.

And some of the biggest changes may happen closer to home. State governors and regulators have a say in electricity prices, while local authorities decide where new homes can be built.

Democrats have won governorships in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, where the rising cost of electricity for data centres is already a concern.

The hardest test may be something Democrats cannot fix with a new law.

The price of petrol, diesel and fertiliser will depend heavily on what happens in the war with Iran. And forecasters disagree about what comes next.

Oxford Economics warns that with the election over, further escalation "appears more likely," because petrol prices will no longer be as much of a political concern for the president.

And here's the awkward part. If prices come down, Democrats may struggle to claim the credit. If they go up, they may share the blame.

There is an immediate test, too. Government funding runs out after 11 December, and the chances of another shutdown look uncomfortably high.

Democrats won the argument. Now they have to show they can make life cheaper.

#135. Welsh aviation firm AerFin bought in £475m deal

The company said it was beginning a "new chapter"

A Newport aviation company, that has grown into a global supplier of aircraft engines, parts and maintenance services, has been bought in a deal worth about Â£475m.

Japanese firm ORIX Aviation has agreed to acquire AerFin, which employs more than 230 people and operates from sites in the UK and around the world.

Founded by Bob James, AerFin has developed from a south Wales business into a global aviation aftermarket specialist.

Announcing the takeover, AerFin described it as the start of a new chapter for the company.

The firm - which was majority owned by a private equity company prior to the sale - buys, sells, leases and repairs aircraft, engines and components.

In 2025, the company moved into a new purpose-built, 116,000 sq ft facility, external at Indurent Park in Newport.

There are no plans to move its headquarters from Newport.

ORIX Aviation is the aircraft leasing arm of Japanese financial services giant the ORIX Group.

Simon Goodson, AerFin's chief executive, said the deal would help grow the business

Chief executive Simon Goodson called the acquisition a "defining moment" in the company's history.

"I am delighted that AerFin is joining the ORIX Group, a business that shares our values and belief in trusted partnerships, flexible solutions and finding the way ahead for our customers," he said.

He said support from the wider ORIX Group would provide "the confidence and platform" to grow the aviation business.

"This is a defining moment in AerFin's story and the beginning of an exciting new chapter," said Goodson.

Aerfin helps airlines and aviation companies source parts and manage assets

AerFin has built its existing business around extending the life of aircraft and engines through trading, leasing, repair and reuse.

It helps airlines and aviation companies source parts and manage assets. The company claims to serve more than 600 organisations.

AerFin moved into a new purpose-built facility in Newport last year

For ORIX Aviation, the acquisition expands its activities beyond aircraft ownership and leasing, into aftermarket services and end-of-life aircraft management.

The company said the combination would strengthen its capabilities across the entire lifecycle of an aircraft.

James Meyler, chief executive of ORIX Aviation, said AerFin had built "a leading aviation aftermarket platform" supported by extensive technical expertise and an international customer base.

The sale also marks the exit of Danish private equity investor CataCap, which had been the majority shareholder in AerFin.

Founder Bob James said he was pleased to see the business move to what he described as "a long-term home", capable of supporting AerFin's next phase of expansion.

"I recognise a strong leadership team and a long-term home when I see one," James said.

#134. Nvidia's Huang is asked to brief Congress on reported Chinese spy relationship

Three House Republican committee leaders asked Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang to sit for a closed-door briefing on his relationship with a tech executive reportedly identified as a Chinese intelligence asset.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information," the GOP lawmakers wrote in a letter to Huang unveiled Thursday, using the acronym for the People's Republic of China.

They are seeking information on Huang's interactions with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's "security protocols against foreign adversary espionage," they wrote in the brief message, which is dated Wednesday.

The letter does not specify which reports it is referring to. But The New York Times reported last week that U.S. intelligence found Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen determined to be funneling information to Beijing, had "developed a close relationship" with Jensen Huang.

President Donald Trump, who himself has forged close ties with Jensen Huang as he champions the U.S. artificial intelligence industry, was briefed about a year ago on those intelligence findings, the Times reported.

Trump "chose not to do anything with the information," according to the newspaper, which cited interviews with people briefed on the surveillance and other sources.

The letter to Huang was signed by House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast, R-Fla., Select Committee on China Chairman John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence Chairman Rick Crawford, R-Ark.

Nvidia did not directly respond when asked by CNBC to comment on the letter and confirm whether Huang would attend the lawmakers' proposed briefing.

Nvidia instead provided a statement that largely mirrored the one it previously sent the Times, in which a spokesperson suggested Jensen Huang's interactions with Alice Huang were no different from his meetings with other executive clients.

"Our regional cloud customers receive ordinary business information — not sensitive NVIDIA trade secrets—and zero information relevant to national security," the spokesperson said in the statement.

"Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year," the spokesperson said. "We're confident that if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know."

A spokeswoman for Mast did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for additional information. Megaspeed did not immediately respond to an email seeking comment.

The lawmakers invited the Nvidia CEO to appear for an in-person joint briefing on Nov. 18. Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17.

"China's access to that sensitive U.S. tech is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress," the committee leaders wrote. "It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk."

#132. Pound rises to its highest level since the Brexit referendum

The pound has risen to its highest overall level since the Brexit referendum over a decade ago.

Sterling has risen notably in recent weeks against the euro and the dollar, ahead of an expected rise in interest rates.

The most-watched measure of the value of the British currency against a basket of 18 of the world's main currencies this week reached its joint highest level since the June 2016 referendum, according to Bank of England figures.

The value reached 86.4 on an index where 100 equals the value in 2005. The Bank will officially confirm the calculation on Thursday.

The value of sterling fell significantly after the Brexit referendum in June 2016, and reached further lows during the Brexit process, the Covid pandemic and the 2022 mini-Budget.

Some traders said the UK was being seen in a different light given relative political stability, solid growth and the rapprochement with the European Union.

Sterling has responded strongly to Brexit developments and political uncertainty in recent years.

Jim O'Neill, an economist and former Treasury minister, said: "I'm not surprised. People were, and to a lesser extent, are, underestimating the improving momentum in the UK."

A stronger pound can help rein in inflation, and increase buying power for consumers on imports and on holidays.

UK growth so far this year has outperformed all forecasts, but there are signs that higher energy and fuel prices have now hit consumer confidence.

The Bank's nine-member Monetary Policy Committee faces an important decision on interest rates on Thursday, with the first rise in three years widely expected.

The pound was up 0.13% against the dollar at $1.3495 and 0.16% lower against the euro at â¬1.1979 as of 04:15 GMT on Thursday.

#129. IRS unveils higher capital gains tax brackets for 2027

The IRS has unveiled higher capital gains tax brackets for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency boosted the taxable income limits for the long-term capital gains brackets, which apply to assets owned for more than one year.

It also increased figures for dozens of other provisions, including federal income tax brackets, the estate and gift tax exemption, and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

Despite post-election stock market volatility, many long-term investors hold profitable assets after years of market growth.

The new capital gains brackets could impact investors' 2027 plans for sales or rebalancing, which can trigger taxes for brokerage account investments.

The latest round of IRS inflation adjustments for 2027 come as many Americans wrestle with affordability amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

The capital gains rate you pay — 0%, 15% or 20% — is based on which bracket you fall into based on taxable income.

You calculate taxable income by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income.

For 2027, the standard deduction will rise to $16,600 for single filers, up from $16,100, and $33,200 for married couples filing jointly, up from $32,200.

Starting in 2027, single filers with taxable income of $51,050 or less and married couples filing jointly with $102,100 or less, will qualify for the 0% long-term capital gains rate.

#128. Second buyer makes rescue bid for Poundland as retailer faces collapse

A second buyer has made a rescue bid for Poundland as the discount retailer teeters on the brink of collapse, with thousands of jobs at risk.

London-based Xyra Group has offered to pay off half of Poundland's outstanding debts - a key sticking point in takeover talks so far - immediately. The rest would be deferred.

With Poundland close to collapse, US-based owners Gordon Brothers have already rejected a rival bid from an Irish property group which would have saved its 12,000 staff.

Gordon Brothers said the business has faced years of instability and "remains challenged and vulnerable".

Sources familiar with the offer said Xyra is offering to immediately pay off Â£12.5m of an outstanding Â£30m loan on Poundland's books.

The offer came as Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds held crunch talks with Gordon Brothers about the future of the business, urging the investment firm to protect staff however possible.

Gordon Brothers, which bought Poundland for Â£1 last June, committed at the meeting to prioritise offers which protect its shops and workers.

The company said: "We are pursuing all avenues through the sale process to find a solution which protects as many jobs as possible and preserves value. Work continues at pace, engaging in active discussions on offers with interested parties."

Xyra is offering to pay off a higher proportion of Poundland's outstanding debts than Martin Property Group, which previously submitted a bid that Gordon Brothers rejected.

But doubts remain about the credibility of Xyra's offer, as there is little evidence they meet Gordon Brothers' tests, including whether they have the money and whether they would be good long-term owners of Poundland.

Sources pointed to the appointment of Andrew Brode to Xyra's board as a sign of its credibility. Brode works for RWS - a company that provides AI solutions for businesses.

Judith MacKenzie, head of Downing Fund Managers, told the BBC Brode is "well known and very credible" among small businesses in the UK.

But she said there is "hardly any information" publicly available about Xyra. "So, although some of the people look good, I can't find any evidence to say that they've got the money to back it up yet," MacKenzie added.

Gordon Brothers said: "Our intent throughout the comprehensive sale process has been to protect as many jobs as possible and preserve value in order to deliver the best available outcome for all the people, creditors and local communities who care about this business as much as we do."

Both Reynolds and Prime Minister Andy Burnham have questioned why a deal for the future of Poundland has not yet been made.

Though Xyra made an offer to take over the whole business, it is unclear whether it would keep Poundland together as its owner or break the company up.

Several Poundland suppliers have said they would also go bust if the retailer collapses. Its founder Steve Smith said two or three thousand suppliers to the firm, those who solely work for Poundland, would be caught up in the collapse.

He added that not only would Poundland's 12,000 staff be affected in that case, but potentially up to 30,000 overall.

#125. Drunk and dangerous e-bike riders face on-the-spot fines under crackdown

People riding drunk or dangerously on e-bikes â and those who jump red lights - could face on-the-spot fines under plans to curb anti-social behaviour.

Firms which run shared e-bike schemes will also be expected to manage their fleets better and provide designated parking areas to keep pavements safe.

They could also be required to take out insurance so people can claim compensation if they are hit by someone on a shared bike.

It's part of a crackdown on what ministers called the "wild west" of e-biking â with a patchwork of rules across the country often leaving the streets unsafe for pedestrians.

A consultation launched on Thursday sets out proposed national standards to make journeys safer for everyone and keep pavements clear, particularly for wheelchair users and families with prams.

The first e-bikes were launched in Exeter in 2016 and arrived in London a year later, growing to become a popular commuter option.

According to the Greater London Authority, 37 million trips were taken on dockless e-bikes last year â a third more than the previous year.

Shared e-bikes - such as Lime, Voi, Beryl, Dott, Forest and Bolt - make short journeys quick and convenient.

While some areas enforce rules that mean shared bikes can only be left in specific areas, others have looser rules.

Under the government's proposals, local leaders will be able to license schemes in their area, allowing them to enforce minimum standards and better integrate e-bikes into existing transport networks.

Measures could include requiring operators to have a parking plan and to manage fleet sizes and operating areas, so bikes aren't overcrowding streets.

The consultation will also consider ways to tackle antisocial and illegal behaviour, to prevent users from riding shared cycles drunk, underage, or on pavements. There will also be clear consequences set out â such as fines - for this type of behaviour.

The government is also consulting on whether local leaders should have extra powers, including setting fees and requirements for operators to share data to improve enforcement and journey planning.

The consultation will last for eight weeks after which the government will draw up legislation to implement the changes.

'Dumped e-scooters force blind people into road'

Red light jumping cyclists offered film over fine

'Our lives will never be the same again after Lime bike crash'

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the e-bike clampdown was a "small but important everyday fix" which would make streets safer.

Transport Secretary Heidi Alexander said the government wanted e-bike schemes to "grow in popularity" but acknowledged they were "causing a little bit of nuisance at the moment".

Asked how punishments for dangerous cycling could be enforced, Alexander said licensing rules could allow for fines but added: "What you can't do is legislate for every eventuality, you can't legislate for idiots."

"The police enforce the rules of the road to stop cyclists when they are behaving recklessly but police have got a lot on their plate so individuals have to take some responsibility," she added.

Bosses at Lime, Voi and Bolt all welcomed the proposals, adding that consistent standards being applied across the country would provide clarity and certainty for operators, riders and the general public.

Mike Wordingham from the Royal National Institute for the Blind said 92% of blind and partially sighted people had been forced to walk into roads due to e-bikes blocking pavements.

He welcomed the government's announced but warned that tackling rider behaviour would be "difficult" and said a locally-led approach shouldn't lead to "a patchwork of regulations and inconsistency in implementing them".

#122. IRS announces new federal income tax brackets for 2027

The IRS has announced new federal income tax brackets and standard deductions for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency raised the income thresholds for each bracket, which apply to tax year 2027 for returns filed in 2028.

The IRS also increased figures for other key provisions, including long-term capital gains brackets, the estate and gift tax exemption, and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

For 2027, the top rate of 37% applies to individuals with taxable income above $661,375 and married couples filing jointly earning $793,650 or more.

Federal income tax brackets show how much you owe on each part of your taxable income, which you calculate by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income.

The standard deduction will also increase in 2027, rising to $33,200 for married couples filing jointly, up from $32,200 in 2026. Starting in 2027, single filers can claim $16,600, a bump up from $16,100.

The new tax brackets come as Americans wrestle with affordability issues amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

The consumer price index climbed 3.7% in September 2026 from one year earlier, up from a yearly pace of 3.4% in August, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported in October.

The U.S. federal income tax system is progressive, which means tax rates increase as earnings rise.

For 2027, the federal brackets include seven income tiers, with rates ranging from 10% to 37%. The tax rates apply to each band of income rather than a flat percentage for all earnings.

The IRS adjusts the income ranges for each tax rate yearly based on the consumer price index.

Your federal tax brackets depend on your filing status, which you choose on your tax return. The options include single, married filing jointly, married filing separately, head of household or qualifying surviving spouse.

You can find your 2025 taxable income on line 15 of your individual federal income tax return, known as Form 1040.

#121. Three ways the interest rate rise could affect you

Interest rates have been raised for the first time in more than three years - and it could be good and bad news for you.

Here are three ways the increase to 4% from 3.75% could affect you.

Rate-setters at the Bank make changes to interest rates to fulfil their role of keeping price rises at a steady level.

Higher rates are meant to encourage people to spend less, reducing demand for goods and services and limiting price rises.

This impacts businesses in two ways - less money to spend among their customers may mean they sell less, but also the cost of borrowing to invest as a company goes up.

Many businesses, particularly small ones, have variable rate loans which are influenced by the Bank rate.

Firms say higher rates limit their ability to offer new jobs and wage rises.

Mitchell Barnes, founder and chief executive of Ryse 3D, which manufactures 3D parts for the defence and automotive sectors, said the "reality" of rising interest rates was to look at the cost of employees and people.

That could include greater use of robots rather than workers.

"It's going to cause issues with, I suppose, employment across all sectors for people," he said.

"You're going to see reduced employment, more automation, more robotics being put into facilities, just in order to increase efficiency and make that money go further."

The Bank rate - and where it is expected to go in future - has a significant impact on the interest rates customers are offered on savings products.

So, if you're saving up for something quite expensive, like a holiday, or very expensive, like a home, then a rate rise could be good news.

When interest rates were low throughout the 2010s, the returns for savers were poor, but borrowing costs were low too.

The answer then and now, according to experts, is to make sure you move your savings to benefit from the best possible rates.

What's happening to UK interest rates and what does it mean for mortgages?

You need Â£17,000 for a first home - here's how to save it

Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Leaving your savings in an old account (a product not offered to new customers), rather than shopping around, can mean you are penalised for your loyalty.

"Savers have endured years of unrewarded loyalty, with those leaving [a Â£20,000 cash pot] in closed accounts losing out on an extra Â£139 over the past three years compared to on-sale easy access accounts," said Rachel Springall, from financial information service Moneyfacts.

She also said locking away your money for longer, with a fixed-term deal, is usually more lucrative.

The other flipside is that renting while you save could get more expensive, because a rising Bank rate may well increase costs for landlords.

The big losers from a rate rise are those who borrow money, and specifically those on variable rate mortgages.

About 500,000 homeowners have a mortgage that "tracks" the Bank of England's rate.

Any rise in the Bank rate means an increase in the monthly repayments on their outstanding loan. For a typical loan, the cost is going up by about Â£31 a month now the Bank has raised the base rate, but it depends on the size of people's loans.

An additional 500,000 homeowners on standard variable rates (SVRs) must wait to see if their lender chooses to pass on any Bank rate rise. Typically, this would mean a Â£14 increase per month in repayments for a 0.25 percentage points rates rise.

If you, like 87% of mortgage-holders, have a fixed-rate deal, then the payments will stay the same until the end of your current deal, which is usually two or five years.

While monthly payments are not immediately affected by a rate change, future deals are. Lenders had already "priced in" the latest rate rise when quoting new deals. Expectations for the Bank rate into next year are crucial on what they offer next.

The average rate on fixed deals are much higher now than was predicted at the start of the year. It means those remortgaging, possibly after the post-Covid boom in the housing market, are facing the prospect of higher mortgage payments.

Some people have been choosing to move onto tracker deals instead, taking a view - including some risk - of the Bank rate not moving towards a series of further hikes.

Do you have a mortgage, a credit card, a car finance agreement? How are interest rates affecting you?

#117. IRS announces bigger estate and gift tax exemption for 2027

The Internal Revenue Service has announced a higher estate and gift tax exemption for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency increased the federal "basic exclusion amount" to $15.48 million per person for 2027, up from $15 million in 2026. The exemption applies to tax-free transfers during life and at death.

State estate and inheritance tax exemptions can be lower.

The IRS also increased figures for dozens of other provisions, including federal income tax brackets, the capital gains tax brackets and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

The inflation adjustments for 2027 come as Americans wrestle with affordability issues amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill" increased the estate and gift tax exemption to $15 million per person. Without the change from Congress, the exemption would have reverted to 2017 levels — $5 million plus an inflation adjustment — after 2025, which spurred proactive estate planning from ultra-wealthy families before the legislation.

Under current law, few families are subject to federal estate taxes. For 2026, families will file about 6,890 federal estate tax returns, and roughly 3,900 will be taxable, according to estimates from the Tax Policy Center, a think tank.

#116. The engine parts that 'fight the laws of the universe'

A jet engine will have hundreds of turbine blades

Every time you fly, an extraordinary feat of endurance occurs inside the aircraft's engines.

Hidden from view inside the sleek outer nacelle are hundreds of little turbine blades that can shrug off enormous mechanical stresses and temperatures exceeding 1,500C.

It is a true engineering marvel. And these hardy blades are not sculpted or cut from their material â they are grown.

During the 1960s, engineers worked out how to take a molten metal alloy, cast it into the shape of a turbine blade, and then cool it very carefully from one end to the other over a matter of hours. A single crystal or grain of metal alloy forms during this process of gradual, delicate solidification.

Like turning a cup of water into one big snowflake, of your own design, rather than a mini blizzard of many smaller snowflakes.

"It's about as complicated as manufacturing gets," says Ben Fifoot, manufacturing engineer at Rolls-Royce, which makes turbine blades in the UK. "It's a real motivator to get out of bed in the morning."

Only a handful of companies around the world can produce these single crystal turbine blades, and the process has been perfected end-to-end in just five countries, including the US and UK. Engine-makers continually vie for access to the technology.

Rolls-Royce, GE and France's Safran are among the few firms that have perfected the process.

In September, GE Aerospace announced, external it would acquire its turbine blade supplier for $11.75bn (Â£8.78bn). Neither company responded to BBC News' requests for comment but the move has been described as an effort to ensure supply of these critical components.

Separately, in March, Rolls-Royce said it would invest more than Â£19m in its Rotherham blade casting facility.

Turbine blade specialists continue to work on improving the capabilities of this little-known technology. All the while, China is desperate to prove that its engineers can produce turbine blades of the same quality as those in the West. Who will come out on top?

"Demand for [turbine blades] is huge. So few companies have managed to do it, which does give it this kind of magical aura," says Bill Sweetman, an aviation industry writer and consultant.

He suspects GE Aerospace's recent move is about securing reliability and predictability in the notoriously volatile aircraft manufacturing supply chain.

The casts play a crucial part in the cooling process

But why is the single crystal casting process, which has been around for decades now, still so specialist?

To grow one of these blades, asserts Fifoot, is to "fight the laws of the universe".

This is because each molten blade would naturally cool into a solid object consisting of many small grains of metal alloy, all jumbled together. But you don't want a crumbly sugar cube, you want a perfect diamond.

Getting a single crystal to grow into the shape of the blade instead isn't just a matter of cooling the molten version down slowly, the process also uses a bizarre trick called a pigtail.

The pigtail is a small spiral at one end of the blade. Fifoot says that, at the beginning of the cooling process, this spiral "chokes" the flow of solidifying crystals so that just one takes over. A bit like a hairpin corner in an F1 race right before the finish line â one driver can take the lead here and go on to win.

"Sometimes we need to play around with the number of turns or the angles on [the pigtail] to try and get more favourable grain," adds Fifoot. Once the blade is formed, this crucial but sacrificial spiral is cut off.

France's Safran is among the few companies that can make single crystal blades

Blades made by Rolls-Royce are used in aircraft engines including the Trent XWB-84, which powers the Airbus A350-900, and the Trent 1000 XE, for the Boeing 787.

The single crystal is essential to ensure each blade's performance. Sometimes a blade doesn't cool quite as planned, for example two crystals form within it instead of just one, and the metal alloy must be reprocessed so that the manufacturer may re-use the material.

Special ceramic coatings also protect blades from extreme temperatures inside engines. And another important feature is that blades are cast with tiny channels that allow a flow of cooler air to coat them in a kind of film while the aircraft engine is working.

"The drive at the moment [across the industry] is currently to make those cooling passages really intricate," says Paul Withey, at the University of Birmingham, who formerly worked for Rolls-Royce. The more turbulent those little flows of cool air are, he explains, the more they will knock heat out of the surrounding, much hotter air.

And blade-makers also seek to reduce the mass of these components as much as possible.

"If we can take any weight out of it, you can run that engine more efficiently using less fuel â you can spin it faster and hotter," says Fifoot.

The turbine blades are used in the Trent 1000 engine among others

Meanwhile, China hopes to become only the sixth country to master the whole technology chain for single crystal blade casting.

A report in May, external quoted one engineer at the AECC Beijing Institute of Aeronautical Materials claiming that China now had a single crystal alloy "superior to or equivalent to the second-generation single-crystal superalloys widely used in Europe and the US". It costs less to make, too, apparently.

But the West has a "huge advantage" over China given that the majority of commercial airliner production happens there, says Sweetman. Supply chains and the latest innovations are generally clustered in the West. Plus, Western manufacturers also have lengthy safety records under their belts.

China must compete on practically every aspect of aircraft manufacturing, external â by no means just on turbine blades â in order to make serious in-roads. "You can definitely see that happening but it's a long, uphill struggle," adds Sweetman.

Demand for aircraft is currently high, which is already straining various supply chains in the industry. That demand could grow higher still, suggests Withey.

During the next few years, one of the two main aircraft manufacturers, Airbus and Boeing, could launch a new short-haul plane, external, he says. Then, the other would have to follow suit in order to remain competitive.

"There'll be a bun fight," says Withey, over who gets to supply the engines and, by extension, those all-important single crystal turbine blades.
